<span class="atom-split" data-atom="the_merry_wives_of_windsor" data-title="THE MERRY WIVES OF WINDSOR" data-chapter="" data-kind="intro"></span>


THE MERRY WIVES OF WINDSOR
A LEGEND dating from 1702 claims that Shakespeare wrote The Merry Wives of Windsor in fourteen days and by command of [[characters/queen-elizabeth|Queen Elizabeth]]; in 1709 she was said to have wished particularly to see [[characters/falstaff|Falstaff]] in [[motifs/love|love]]. Whether or not this is true, a passage towards the end of the play alluding directly to the ceremonies of the Order of the Garter, Britain’s highest order of chivalry, encourages the belief that the play has a direct connection with a specific occasion. In 1597 George Carey, Lord Hunsdon, Lord Chamberlain and patron of Shakespeare’s company, was installed at Windsor as a Knight of the Garter. The [[characters/queen|Queen]] was not present at the installation but had attended the Garter Feast at the Palace of Westminster on St George’s Day (23 April). Shakespeare’s play was probably performed in association with this occasion, and may have been written especially for it. It was first printed, in a corrupt text, in 1602; a better text appears in the 1623 Folio.
Some of the characters—Sir John [[characters/falstaff|Falstaff]], [[characters/mistress-quickly|Mistress Quickly]], [[characters/pistol|Pistol]], Nim, [[characters/justice-shallow|Justice Shallow]]—appear also in I and 2 [[characters/henry-iv|Henry IV]] and [[characters/henry-v|Henry V]], but in spite of a reference to ’the wild Prince and Poins’ at 3.2.66-7, this is essentially an Elizabethan [[forms/comedy|comedy]], the only one that Shakespeare set firmly in England. The play is full of details that would have been familiar to Elizabethan Londoners, and the language is colloquial and up to date. The plot, however, is made up of conventional situations whose ancestry is literary rather than realistic. There are many analogues to Shakespeare’s basic plot situations in medieval and other tales, some in books that he probably or certainly knew. The central story, of Sir John’s unsuccessful attempts to seduce [[characters/mistress-page|Mistress Page]] and [[characters/mistress-ford|Mistress Ford]], and of [[characters/master-ford|Master Ford]]’s unfounded [[concepts/jealousy|jealousy]], is in the tradition of the Italian novella, and may have been suggested by Ser Giovanni Fiorentino’s II Pecorone (1558). Alongside it Shakespeare places the comical but finally [[concepts/romantic-love|romantic love]] story of [[characters/anne-page|Anne Page]], wooed by the foolish but rich Abraham Slender and the irascible French [[characters/doctor-caius|Doctor Caius]], but won by the young and handsome [[characters/fenton|Fenton]]. The play contains a higher proportion of prose to verse than any other play by Shakespeare, and the action is often broadly comic; but it ends, after the midnight scene in Windsor Forest during which Sir John is frightened out of his lechery, in [[concepts/forgiveness|forgiveness]] and [[motifs/love|love]].
The Merry Wives of Windsor is known to have been acted for James I on 4 November 1604, and for Charles I in 1638. It was revived soon after the theatres reopened, in 1660; at first it was not particularly popular, but since 1720 it has consistently pleased audiences. Many artists have illustrated it, and it forms the basis for a number of operas, including Otto Nicolai’s Die lustigen Weiber von Windsor (1849) and Giuseppe Verdi’s comic masterpiece, [[characters/falstaff|Falstaff]] (1893).

| PAGE | We three to hear it, and end it between them. |

| SIR JOHN | [[characters/pistol\|Pistol]]. |
| [[characters/pistol\|PISTOL]] | He hears with ears. |

| SIR JOHN | Is this true, [[characters/pistol\|Pistol]]? |

| SLENDER | (pointing to Nim) By these gloves, then, ’twas he. |

| SLENDER | By this hat, then, he in the red face had it. For though I cannot remember what I did when you made me drunk, yet I am not altogether an ass. |
| SIR JOHN | (to Bardolph) What say you, Scarlet and John? |

| EVANS | So Got ’udge me, that is a virtuous mind. |
| SIR JOHN | You hear all these matters denied, gentlemen, you hear it. |

| SLENDER | Nay, I will do as my cousin Shallow says. I pray you pardon me. He’s a Justice of Peace in his country, simple though I stand here. |

| SLENDER | Why, if it be so, I will marry her upon any reasonable demands. |

| SHALLOW | Cousin Abraham Slender, can you [[motifs/love\|love]] her? |
| SLENDER | I [[concepts/hope\|hope]], sir, I will do as it shall become one that would do reason. |
| EVANS | Nay, Got’s lords and his ladies, you must speak positable if you can carry her your desires towards her. |
| SHALLOW | That you must. Will you, upon good dowry, marry her? |

| SHALLOW | Nay, conceive me, conceive me, sweet coz. What I do is to pleasure you, coz. Can you [[motifs/love\|love]] the maid? |
| SLENDER | I will marry her, sir, at your request. But if there be no great [[motifs/love\|love]] in the beginning, yet heaven may decrease it upon better acquaintance, when we are married and have more occasion to know one another. I [[concepts/hope\|hope]] upon familiarity will grow more contempt. But if you say ‘marry her’, I will marry her. That I am freely dissolved, and dissolutely. |
| EVANS | It is a fery discretion answer, save the faul’ is in the ’ord ‘dissolutely’. The ’ort is, according to our meaning, ‘resolutely’. His meaning is good. |
| SHALLOW | Ay, I think my cousin meant [[motifs/well\|well]]. |
| SLENDER | Ay, or else I would I might be hanged, la. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/anne-page\|Anne Page]] |
| SHALLOW | Here comes fair Mistress Anne.—Would I were young for your sake, Mistress Anne. |
| ANNE | The dinner is on the table. My father desires your worships’ company. |
| SHALLOW | I will wait on him, fair Mistress Anne. |

| ANNE | (to Slender) Will’t please your worship to come in, sir? |
| SLENDER | No, I thank you, forsooth, heartily; I am very [[motifs/well\|well]]. |
| ANNE | The dinner attends you, sir. |
| SLENDER | I am not a-hungry, I thank you, forsooth. (To Simple) Go, sirrah; for all you are my man, go wait upon my cousin Shallow. Exit Simple A Justice of Peace sometime may be beholden to his friend for a man. I keep but three men and a boy yet, till my mother be dead. But what though? Yet I live like a poor gentleman born. |
| ANNE | I may not go in without your worship. They will not sit till you come. |
| SLENDER | I’[[concepts/faith\|faith]], I’ll eat nothing. I thank you as much as though I did. |
| ANNE | I pray you, sir, walk in. |
| *(didascalia)* | Dogs bark within |
| SLENDER | I had rather walk here, I thank you. I bruised my shin th‘other day, with playing at sword and dagger with a master of fence—three veneys for a dish of stewed prunes—and, by my troth, I cannot abide the smell of hot meat since. Why do your dogs bark so? Be there bears i’th’ town? |
| ANNE | I think there are, sir. I heard them talked of. |
| SLENDER | I [[motifs/love\|love]] the sport [[motifs/well\|well]]—but I shall as soon quarrel at it as any man in England. You are afraid if you see the bear loose, are you not? |
| ANNE | Ay, indeed, sir. |
| SLENDER | That’s meat and drink to me, now. I have seen Sackerson loose twenty times, and have taken him by the chain. But I warrant you, the women have so cried and shrieked at it that it passed. But women, indeed, cannot abide ’em. They are very ill-favoured, rough things. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Page |
| PAGE | Come, gentle Master Slender, come. We stay for you. |
| SLENDER | I’ll eat nothing, I thank you, sir. |
| PAGE | By cock and pie, you shall not choose, sir. Come, come. |
| SLENDER | Nay, pray you lead the way. |
| PAGE | Come on, sir. |
| SLENDER | Mistress Anne, yourself shall go first. |
| ANNE | Not I, sir. Pray you keep on. |
| SLENDER | Truly, I will not go first, truly, la. I will not do you that wrong. |
| ANNE | I pray you, sir. |
| SLENDER | I’ll rather be unmannerly than troublesome. You do yourself wrong, indeed, la. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt Slender first, the others following |

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Sir John [[characters/falstaff\|Falstaff]], Bardolph, Nim, [[characters/pistol\|Pistol]], and Robin |
| SIR JOHN | Mine Host of the Garter! |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the Host of the Garter |
| HOST | What says my bully rook? Speak scholarly and wisely. |
| SIR JOHN | Truly, mine Host, I must turn away some of my followers. |
| HOST | Discard, bully Hercules, cashier. Let them wag. Trot, trot. |
| SIR JOHN | I sit at ten pounds a week. |
| HOST | Thou’rt an emperor: Caesar, kaiser, and pheezer. I will entertain Bardolph. He shall draw, he shall tap. Said I [[motifs/well\|well]], bully [[characters/hector\|Hector]]? 11 |
| SIR JOHN | Do so, good mine Host. |
| HOST | I have spoke; let him follow. (To Bardolph) Let me see thee froth and lime. I am at a word: follow. Exit |
| SIR JOHN | Bardolph, follow him. A tapster is a good trade. An old cloak makes a new jerkin; a withered servingman a fresh tapster. Go; adieu. |
| BARDOLPH | It is a life that I have desired. I will thrive. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit |
| [[characters/pistol\|PISTOL]] | O base Hungarian wight, wilt thou the spigot wield? |
| NIM | He was gotten in drink; his mind is not heroic. Is not the humour conceited? |
| SIR JOHN | I am glad I am so acquit of this tinderbox. His thefts were too open. His filching was like an unskilful singer: he kept not [[motifs/time\|time]].<br>Him The good humour is to steal at a minute’s rest. |
| [[characters/pistol\|PISTOL]] | ‘Convey’ the wise it call. ‘Steal’? Foh, a fico for the<br>phrase! |
| SIR JOHN | [[motifs/well\|Well]], sirs, I am almost out at heels. |
| [[characters/pistol\|PISTOL]] | Why then, let kibes ensue. |
| SIR JOHN | There is no remedy: I must cony-catch, I must shift. |
| [[characters/pistol\|PISTOL]] | Young ravens must have food. |
| SIR JOHN | Which of you know Ford of this town? |
| [[characters/pistol\|PISTOL]] | I ken the wight. He is of substance good. |
| SIR JOHN | My honest lads, I will tell you what I am about. |
| [[characters/pistol\|PISTOL]] | Two yards and more. |
| SIR JOHN | No quips now, [[characters/pistol\|Pistol]]. Indeed, I am in the waist two yards about. But I am now about no waste; I am about thrift. Briefly, I do mean to make [[motifs/love\|love]] to Ford’s wife. I spy entertainment in her. She discourses, she carves, she gives the leer of invitation. I can construe the action of her familiar style; and the hardest voice of her behaviour, to be Englished rightly, is ‘I am Sir John [[characters/falstaff\|Falstaff]]’s’. |
| [[characters/pistol\|PISTOL]] | He hath studied her [[motifs/well\|well]], and translated her will: out of honesty, into English. |
| NIM | The anchor is deep. Will that humour pass? |
| SIR JOHN | Now, the report goes, she has all the rule of her husband’s purse; he hath a legion of angels. |
| [[characters/pistol\|PISTOL]] | As many devils entertain, and ‘To her, boy!’ say I. |
| NIM | The humour rises; it is good. [[works/humour-(chesterton)\|Humour]] me the angels! |
| SIR JOHN | (showing letters) I have writ me here a letter to her—and here another to Page’s wife, who even now gave me good eyes too, examined my parts with most judicious oeillades; sometimes the beam of her view gilded my foot, sometimes my portly belly. |
| [[characters/pistol\|PISTOL]] | Then did the sun on dunghill shine. |
| NIM | I thank thee for that humour. |
| SIR JOHN | O, she did so course o’er my exteriors, with such a greedy intention, that the appetite of her eye did seem to scorch me up like a burning-glass! Here’s another letter to her. She bears the purse too. She is a region in Guiana, all gold and bounty. I will be cheaters to them both, and they shall be exchequers to me. They shall be my East and West Indies, and I will trade to them both. (Giving a letter to [[characters/pistol\|Pistol]]) Go bear thou this letter to [[characters/mistress-page\|Mistress Page]], (giving a letter to Nim) and thou this to [[characters/mistress-ford\|Mistress Ford]]. We will thrive, lads, we will thrive. |
| [[characters/pistol\|PISTOL]] | (returning the letter)<br>Shall I Sir [[characters/pandarus\|Pandarus]] of Troy become,<br>And by my side wear steel? Then [[characters/lucifer\|Lucifer]] take all. |
| NIM | (returning the letter) I will run no base humour. Here, take the humour-letter. I will keep the haviour of reputation. |
| SIR JOHN | (to Robin)<br>Hold, sirrah. Bear you these letters tightly.<br>Sail like my pinnace to these golden shores.<br>He gives Robin the letters<br>Rogues, hence, avaunt! Vanish like hailstones! Go!<br>Trudge, plod, away o’th’ hoof, seek shelter, pack!<br>[[characters/falstaff\|Falstaff]] will learn the humour of the age:<br>French thrift, you rogues—myself and skirted page. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt Sir John and Robin |
| [[characters/pistol\|PISTOL]] | Let vultures gripe thy guts!—for gourd and fullam<br>holds,<br>And high and low beguiles the rich and poor.<br>Tester I’ll have in pouch when thou shalt lack,<br>Base Phrygian Turk! |
| NIM | I have operations which be humours of [[concepts/revenge\|revenge]]. |
| [[characters/pistol\|PISTOL]] | Wilt thou [[concepts/revenge\|revenge]]? |
| NIM | By welkin and her [[motifs/stars\|stars]]! |
| [[characters/pistol\|PISTOL]] | With wit or steel? |
| NIM | With both the humours, I.<br>I will discuss the humour of this [[motifs/love\|love]] to Ford. |
| [[characters/pistol\|PISTOL]] | And I to Page shall eke unfold<br>How [[characters/falstaff\|Falstaff]], varlet vile,<br>His dove will prove, his gold will hold,<br>And his soft couch defile. |
| NIM | My humour shall not cool. I will incense Ford to deal with [[motifs/poison\|poison]]; I will possess him with yellowness; for this revolt of mine is dangerous. That is my true humour. |
| [[characters/pistol\|PISTOL]] | Thou art the Mars of malcontents.<br>I second thee. Troop on. Exeunt |

### Atto 1, Scena 4

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/mistress-quickly\|Mistress Quickly]] and Simple |
| [[characters/mistress-quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | What, John Rugby! |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter John Rugby |
| [[characters/mistress-quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | I pray thee, go to the casement and see if you can see<br>my master, Master [[characters/doctor-caius\|Doctor Caius]], coming. If he do,<br>i’[[concepts/faith\|faith]], and find anybody in the house, here will be an<br>old abusing of God’s patience and the [[archetypes/king\|King]]’s English. |
| RUGBY | I’ll go watch. |
| [[characters/mistress-quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | Go; and we’ll have a posset for’t soon at [[motifs/night\|night]], in [[concepts/faith\|faith]], at the latter end of a seacoal [[motifs/fire\|fire]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit Rugby |
| [[characters/mistress-quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | An honest, willing, kind fellow as ever servant shall come in house withal; and, I warrant you, no telltale, nor no breedbate. His worst fault is that he is given to [[motifs/prayer\|prayer]]; he is something peevish that way—but nobody but has his fault. But let that pass. Peter Simple you say your name is? |
| SIMPLE | Ay, for fault of a better. |
| [[characters/mistress-quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | And Master Slender’s your master ? |
| SIMPLE | Ay, forsooth. |
| [[characters/mistress-quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | Does he not wear a great round beard, like a glover’s paring-knife? |
| SIMPLE | No, forsooth; he hath but a little whey face, with a little yellow beard, a [[characters/cain\|Cain]]-coloured beard. |
| [[characters/mistress-quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | A softly spirited man, is he not? |
| SIMPLE | Ay, forsooth; but he is as tall a man of his hands as any is between this and his head. He hath fought with a warrener. |
| [[characters/mistress-quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | How say you?—O, I should remember him: does he not hold up his head, as it were, and strut in his gait? |
| SIMPLE | Yes, indeed does he. |
| [[characters/mistress-quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | [[motifs/well\|Well]], heaven send [[characters/anne-page\|Anne Page]] no worse fortune! Tell Master Parson Evans I will do what I can for your master. Anne is a good girl, and I wish— |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Rugby |
| *(didascalia)* | RUGBY Out, alas, here comes my master! Exit |
| [[characters/mistress-quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | We shall all be shent. Run in here, good young man; for God’s sake, go into this closet. He will not stay long.<br>Simple steps into the closet<br>What, John Rugby! John! What, John, I say! |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Rugby |
| *(didascalia)* | Speaking loudly Go, John, go enquire for my master. I |
| [[characters/mistress-quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | [[concepts/doubt\|doubt]] he be not [[motifs/well\|well]], that he comes not [[motifs/home\|home]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit Rugby |
| [[characters/mistress-quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | (Singing) ’And down, down, adown-a’ (etc.) |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/doctor-caius\|Doctor Caius]] |
| CAIUS | Vat is you sing? I do not like dese toys. Pray you go and vetch me in my closet un boîtier vert—a box, a green-a box. Do intend vat I speak? A green-a box. |
| [[characters/mistress-quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | Ay, forsooth, I’ll fetch it you. (Aside) I am glad he went not in himself. If he had found the young man, he would have been horn-mad.<br>She goes to fetch the box |
| CAIUS | Fe, fe, fe, fe! Ma foi, il fait fort chaud! Je m’en vais à la cour. La grande affaire. |
| [[characters/mistress-quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | Is it this, sir? |
| CAIUS | Oui. Mets-le à ma pochette. Dépêche, quickly! Vere is dat knave Rugby? |
| [[characters/mistress-quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | What, John Rugby! John! |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Rugby |
| RUGBY | Here, sir. |
| CAIUS | You are John Rugby, and you are Jack Rugby. Come, take-a your rapier, and come after my heel to the [[settings/court\|court]]. |
| RUGBY | ’Tis ready, sir, here in the porch.<br>He fetches the rapier |
| CAIUS | By my trot, I tarry too long. ’Od’s me, qu’ai-j’oublié? Dere is some simples in my closet dat I vill not for the varld I shall leave behind. |
| [[characters/mistress-quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | (aside) Ay me, he’ll find the young man there, and be mad. |
| CAIUS | (discovering Simple) O diable, diable! Vat is in my closet? Villainy, larron! Rugby, my rapier!<br>He takes the rapier |
| [[characters/mistress-quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | Good master, be content. |
| CAIUS | Wherefore shall I be content-a? |
| [[characters/mistress-quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | The young man is an honest man. |
| CAIUS | What shall de honest man do in my closet? Dere is no honest man dat shall come in my closet. |
| [[characters/mistress-quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | I beseech you, be not so phlegmatic. Hear the [[concepts/truth\|truth]] of it. He came of an errand to me from Parson Hugh. |
| CAIUS | Vell. |
| SIMPLE | Ay, forsooth, to [[motifs/desire\|desire]] her to— |
| [[characters/mistress-quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | Peace, I pray you. |
| CAIUS | Peace-a your tongue. (To Simple) Speak-a your tale. |
| SIMPLE | To [[motifs/desire\|desire]] this honest gentlewoman, your maid, to speak a good word to Mistress [[characters/anne-page\|Anne Page]] for my master in the way of [[motifs/marriage\|marriage]]. |
| [[characters/mistress-quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | This is all, indeed, la; but I’ll ne’er put my finger in the [[motifs/fire\|fire]] an need not. |
| CAIUS | Sir Hugh send-a you?—Rugby, baile me some paper.<br>Rugby brings paper<br>(To Simple) Tarry you a little-a while.<br>Caius writes |
| [[characters/mistress-quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | (aside to Simple) I am glad he is so quiet. If he had been throughly moved, you should have heard him so loud and so [[concepts/melancholy\|melancholy]]. But notwithstanding, man, I’ll do your master what good I can. And the very yea and the no is, the French doctor, my master—I may call him my master, look you, for I keep his house, and I wash, wring, brew, bake, scour, dress meat and drink, make the beds, and do all myself— |
| SIMPLE | (aside to [[characters/mistress-quickly\|Mistress Quickly]]) ’Tis a great charge to come under one [[concepts/body\|body]]’s hand. |
| [[characters/mistress-quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | (aside to Simple) Are you advised o’ that? You shall find it a great charge—and to be up early, and down late. But notwithstanding, to tell you in your ear—I would have no words of it—my master himself is in [[motifs/love\|love]] with Mistress [[characters/anne-page\|Anne Page]]. But notwithstanding that, I know Anne’s mind: that’s neither here nor there. |
| CAIUS | (giving the letter to Simple) You, jack’nape, give-a this letter to Sir Hugh. By Gar, it is a shallenge. I will cut his troat in de Park, and I will teach a scurvy jackanape priest to meddle or make. You may be gone. It is not good you tarry here. By Gar, I will cut all his two stones. By Gar, he shall not have a stone to throw at his dog. Exit Simple |
| [[characters/mistress-quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | Alas, he speaks but for his friend. CAIUS It is no matter-a ver dat. Do not you tell-a me dat I shall have [[characters/anne-page\|Anne Page]] for myself? By Gar, I vill kill de jack-priest. And I have appointed mine Host of de Jarteer to measure our weapon. By Gar, I will myself have [[characters/anne-page\|Anne Page]]. |
| [[characters/mistress-quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | Sir, the maid loves you, and all shall be [[motifs/well\|well]]. We must give folks leave to prate, what the goodyear! |
| CAIUS | Rugby, come to the [[settings/court\|court]] with me. (To [[characters/mistress-quickly\|Mistress Quickly]]) By Gar, if I have not [[characters/anne-page\|Anne Page]], I shall turn your head out of my door. Follow my heels, Rugby. |
| [[characters/mistress-quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | You shall have Anne— |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt Caius and Rugby |
| [[characters/mistress-quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | —ass-head of your own. No, I know Anne’s mind for<br>that. Never a woman in Windsor knows more of Anne’s<br>mind than I do, nor can do more than I do with her,<br>I thank heaven. |
| [[characters/fenton\|FENTON]] | (within) Who’s within there, ho! |
| [[characters/mistress-quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | Who’s there, I trow?—Come near the house, I pray you. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Master [[characters/fenton\|Fenton]] |
| [[characters/fenton\|FENTON]] | How now, good woman, how dost thou? |
| [[characters/mistress-quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | The better that it pleases your good worship to ask. |
| [[characters/fenton\|FENTON]] | What news? How does pretty Mistress Anne? |
| [[characters/mistress-quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | In [[concepts/truth\|truth]], sir, and she is pretty, and honest, and gentle, and one that is your friend. I can tell you that by the way, I praise heaven for it. |
| [[characters/fenton\|FENTON]] | Shall I do any good, thinkest thou? Shall I not lose my suit? |
| [[characters/mistress-quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | Troth, sir, all is in His hands above. But notwithstanding, Master [[characters/fenton\|Fenton]], I’ll be sworn on a book she loves you. Have not your worship a wart above your eye? |
| [[characters/fenton\|FENTON]] | Yes, marry, have I. What of that? |
| [[characters/mistress-quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | [[motifs/well\|Well]], thereby hangs a tale. Good [[concepts/faith\|faith]], it is such another Nan!—But I detest, an honest maid as ever broke bread.—We had an hour’s talk of that wart. I shall never laugh but in that maid’s company.—But indeed she is given too much to allicholy and musing.—But for you—[[motifs/well\|well]]—go to! |
| [[characters/fenton\|FENTON]] | [[motifs/well\|Well]], I shall see her today. Hold, there’s money for thee. Let me have thy voice in my behalf. If thou seest her before me, commend me. |
| [[characters/mistress-quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | Will I? I’[[concepts/faith\|faith]], that I will. And I will tell your worship more of the wart the next [[motifs/time\|time]] we have confidence, and of other wooers. |
| [[characters/fenton\|FENTON]] | [[motifs/well\|Well]], farewell. I am in great haste now. |
| [[characters/mistress-quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | Farewell to your worship. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit [[characters/fenton\|Fenton]] |
| [[characters/mistress-quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | Truly, an honest gentleman; but Anne loves him not,<br>for I know Anne’s mind as [[motifs/well\|well]] as another does.—Out<br>upon’t, what have I forgot? Exit |

### Atto 2, Scena 1

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/mistress-page\|Mistress Page]], with a letter |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | What, have I scaped [[motifs/love\|love]]-letters in the holiday [[motifs/time\|time]] of my [[concepts/beauty\|beauty]], and am I now a subject for them? Let me see.<br>She reads<br>‘Ask me no reason why I [[motifs/love\|love]] you, for though [[motifs/love\|Love]] use Reason for his precision, he admits him not for his counsellor. You are not young; no more am I. Go to, then, there’s sympathy. You are merry; so am I. Ha, ha, then, there’s more sympathy. You [[motifs/love\|love]] sack, and so do I. Would you [[motifs/desire\|desire]] better sympathy? Let it suffice thee, [[characters/mistress-page\|Mistress Page]], at the least if the [[motifs/love\|love]] of soldier can suffice, that I [[motifs/love\|love]] thee. I will not say “pity me”—’tis not a soldier-like phrase—but I say “[[motifs/love\|love]] me”.<br>By me, thine own true knight,<br>By day or [[motifs/night\|night]]<br>Or any kind of light, 15<br>With all his might<br>For thee to fight,<br>John [[characters/falstaff\|Falstaff]].’<br>What a Herod of Jewry is this! O, wicked, wicked world! One that is [[motifs/well\|well]]-nigh worn to pieces with age, to show himself a young gallant! What an unweighed behaviour hath this Flemish drunkard picked, i’th’ devil’s name, out of my conversation, that he dares in this manner assay me? Why, he hath not been thrice in my company. What should I say to him? was then frugal of my mirth, heaven forgive me. Why, I’ll exhibit a bill in the Parliament for the putting down of men. O God, that I knew how to be revenged on him! For revenged I will be, as sure as his guts are made of puddings. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/mistress-ford\|Mistress Ford]] |
| [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] | [[characters/mistress-page\|Mistress Page]]! By my [[concepts/faith\|faith]], I was going to your house. |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | And by my [[concepts/faith\|faith]], I was coming to you. You look very ill. |
| [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] | Nay, I’ll ne’er believe that: I have to show to the contrary. |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | [[concepts/faith\|Faith]], but you do, in my mind. |
| [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] | [[motifs/well\|Well]], I do, then. Yet I say I could show you to the contrary. O [[characters/mistress-page\|Mistress Page]], give me some counsel. |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | What’s the matter, woman? |
| [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] | O woman, if it were not for one trifling respect, I could come to such [[concepts/honour\|honour]]! |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | Hang the trifle, woman; take the [[concepts/honour\|honour]]. What is it? Dispense with trifles. What is it? |
| [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] | If I would but go to hell for an eternal moment or so, I could be knighted. |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | What? Thou liest! Sir Alice Ford? These knights will hack, and so thou shouldst not alter the article of thy gentry. |
| [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] | We burn daylight. Here: read, read.<br>She gives [[characters/mistress-page\|Mistress Page]] a letter<br>Perceive how I might be knighted.<br>[[characters/mistress-page\|Mistress Page]] reads<br>I shall think the worse of fat men as long as I have an eye to make difference of men’s liking. And yet he would not swear, praised women’s modesty, and gave such orderly and [[motifs/well\|well]]-behaved reproof to all uncomeliness that I would have sworn his disposition would have gone to the [[concepts/truth\|truth]] of his words. But they do no more adhere and keep place together than the hundred and fifty psalms to the tune of ‘Greensleeves’. What tempest, I trow, threw this whale, with so many tuns of oil in his belly, ashore at Windsor? How shall I be revenged on him? I think the best way were to entertain him with [[concepts/hope\|hope]], till the wicked [[motifs/fire\|fire]] of lust have melted him in his own grease. Did you ever hear the like? |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | Letter for letter, but that the name of Page and Ford differs.<br>She gives [[characters/mistress-ford\|Mistress Ford]] her letter<br>To thy great comfort in this mystery of ill opinions, here’s the twin brother of thy letter. But let thine inherit first, for I protest mine never shall. I warrant he hath a thousand of these letters, writ with blank space for different names—sure, more, and these are of the second edition. He will print them, out of [[concepts/doubt\|doubt]]—for he cares not what he puts into the press when he would put us two. I had rather be a giantess, and lie under Mount Pelion. [[motifs/well\|Well]], I will find you twenty lascivious turtles ere one chaste man. |
| [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] | Why, this is the very same: the very hand, the very words. What doth he think of us? |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | Nay, I know not. It makes me almost ready to wrangle with mine own honesty. I’ll entertain myself like one that I am not acquainted withal; for, sure, unless he know some strain in me that I know not myself, he would never have boarded me in this fury. |
| [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] | ’Boarding’ call you it? I’ll be sure to keep him above deck. |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | So will I. If he come under my hatches, I’ll never to sea again. Let’s be revenged on him. Let’s appoint him a meeting, give him a show of comfort in his suit, and lead him on with a fine baited delay till he hath pawned his horses to mine Host of the Garter. |
| [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] | Nay, I will consent to act any villainy against him that may not sully the chariness of our honesty. O that my husband saw this letter! It would give eternal food to his [[concepts/jealousy\|jealousy]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/master-ford\|Master Ford]] with [[characters/pistol\|Pistol]], and [[characters/master-page\|Master Page]] with Nim |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | Why, look where he comes, and my goodman too. He’s as far from [[concepts/jealousy\|jealousy]] as I am from giving him cause; and that, I [[concepts/hope\|hope]], is an unmeasurable distance. |
| [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] | You are the happier woman. |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | Let’s consult together against this greasy knight. Come hither.<br>They withdraw. |
| FORD | [[motifs/well\|Well]], I [[concepts/hope\|hope]] it be not so. |
| [[characters/pistol\|PISTOL]] | [[concepts/hope\|Hope]] is a curtal dog in some affairs.<br>Sir John affects thy wife. |
| FORD | Why, sir, my wife is not young. |
| [[characters/pistol\|PISTOL]] | He woos both high and low, both rich and poor,<br>Both young and old, one with another, Ford.<br>He loves the gallimaufry, Ford. Perpend. |
| FORD | [[motifs/love\|Love]] my wife? |
| [[characters/pistol\|PISTOL]] | With liver burning hot. Prevent,<br>Or go thou like Sir Actaeon, he,<br>With Ringwood at thy heels.<br>O, odious is the name! |
| FORD | What name, sir? |
| [[characters/pistol\|PISTOL]] | The horn, I say. Farewell.<br>Take heed; have open eye; for thieves do foot by [[motifs/night\|night]].<br>Take heed ere [[motifs/summer\|summer]] comes, or cuckoo-[[motifs/birds\|birds]] do<br>sing.—<br>Away, Sir Corporal Nim!—Believe it, Page; he speaks<br>sense. Exit |
| FORD | (aside) I will be patient. I will find out this. |
| NIM | (to Page) And this is true. I like not the humour of lying. He hath wronged me in some humours. I should have borne the humoured letter to her; but I have a sword, and it shall bite upon my necessity. He loves your wife. There’s the short and the long. My name is Corporal Nim. I speak and I avouch ’tis true.<br>My name is Nim, and [[characters/falstaff\|Falstaff]] loves your wife. Adieu.<br>I [[motifs/love\|love]] not the humour of bread and cheese. Adieu. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit |
| PAGE | (aside) The humour of it, quoth a? Here’s a fellow frights English out of his wits. |
| FORD | (aside) I will seek out [[characters/falstaff\|Falstaff]]. |
| PAGE | (aside) I never heard such a drawling, affecting rogue. |
| FORD | (aside) If I do find it—[[motifs/well\|well]]. |
| PAGE | (aside) I will not believe such a Cathayan though the priest o’th’ town commended him for a true man. |
| FORD | (aside) ’Twas a good, sensible fellow. [[motifs/well\|Well]].<br>[[characters/mistress-page\|Mistress Page]] and [[characters/mistress-ford\|Mistress Ford]] come forward |
| PAGE | How now, Meg? |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | Whither go you, George? Hark you.<br>They talk apart |
| [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] | How now, sweet Frank? Why art thou [[concepts/melancholy\|melancholy]]? |
| FORD | I [[concepts/melancholy\|melancholy]]? I am not [[concepts/melancholy\|melancholy]]. Get you [[motifs/home\|home]], go. |
| [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] | [[concepts/faith\|Faith]], thou hast some crotchets in thy head now. Will you go, [[characters/mistress-page\|Mistress Page]]? |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | Have with you.—You’ll come to dinner, George? |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/mistress-quickly\|Mistress Quickly]] |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | (Aside to [[characters/mistress-ford\|Mistress Ford]]) Look who comes yonder. She shall be our messenger to this paltry knight. |
| [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] | (aside to [[characters/mistress-page\|Mistress Page]]) Trust me, I thought on her. She’ll fit it. |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | (to [[characters/mistress-quickly\|Mistress Quickly]]) You are come to see my daughter Anne? |
| [[characters/mistress-quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | Ay, forsooth; and I pray how does good Mistress Anne? |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | Go in with us and see. We have an hour’s talk with you. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt [[characters/mistress-page\|Mistress Page]], [[characters/mistress-ford\|Mistress Ford]], and [[characters/mistress-quickly\|Mistress Quickly]] |
| PAGE | How now, [[characters/master-ford\|Master Ford]]? |
| FORD | You heard what this knave told me, did you not? |
| PAGE | Yes, and you heard what the other told me? |
| FORD | Do you think there is [[concepts/truth\|truth]] in them? |
| PAGE | Hang ’em, slaves! I do not think the knight would offer it. But these that accuse him in his intent towards our wives are a yoke of his discarded men—very rogues, now they be out of service. |
| FORD | Were they his men? |
| PAGE | Marry, were they. |
| FORD | I like it never the better for that. Does he lie at the Garter? |
| PAGE | Ay, marry, does he. If he should intend this voyage toward my wife, I would turn her loose to him; and what he gets more of her than sharp words, let it lie on my head. |
| FORD | I do not misdoubt my wife, but I would be loath to turn them together. A man may be too confident. I would have nothing lie on my head. I cannot be thus satisfied. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the Host of the Garter |
| PAGE | Look where my ranting Host of the Garter comes. There is either liquor in his pate or money in his purse when he looks so merrily.—How now, mine Host? |
| HOST | God bless you, bully rook, God bless you! Thou’rt a gentleman. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Shallow |
| HOST | Cavaliero Justice, I say! |
| SHALLOW | I follow, mine Host, I follow.—Good even and twenty, good [[characters/master-page\|Master Page]]. [[characters/master-page\|Master Page]], will you go with us? We have sport in hand. |
| HOST | Tell him, Cavaliero Justice, tell him, bully rook. |
| SHALLOW | Sir, there is a fray to be fought between Sir Hugh, the Welsh priest, and Caius, the French doctor. |
| FORD | Good mine Host o’th’ Garter, a word with you. |
| HOST | What sayst thou, my bully rook?<br>They talk apart |
| SHALLOW | (to Page) Will you go with us to behold it? My merry Host hath had the measuring of their weapons, and, I think, hath appointed them contrary places. For, believe me, I hear the parson is no jester. Hark, I will tell you what our sport shall be.<br>They talk apart |
| HOST | (to Ford) Hast thou no suit against my knight, my guest cavaliero? |
| *(didascalia)* | FORD None, I protest. But I’ll give you a pottle of burnt sack to give me recourse to him and tell him my name is Brooke—only for a jest. |
| HOST | My hand, bully. Thou shalt have egress and regress—said I [[motifs/well\|well]]?—and thy name shall be Brooke. It is a merry knight. (To Shallow and Page) Will you go, mijn’heers? |
| SHALLOW | Have with you, mine Host. |
| PAGE | I have heard the Frenchman hath good skill in his rapier. |
| *(didascalia)* | SHALLOW Tut, sir, I could have told you more. In these times you stand on distance—your passes, stoccados, and I know not what. ‘Tis the heart, [[characters/master-page\|Master Page]]; showing his rapier-passes ’tis here, ’tis here. I have seen the [[motifs/time\|time]] with my long sword I would have made you four tall fellows skip like rats. |
| HOST | Here, boys; here, here! Shall we wag? |
| PAGE | Have with you. I had rather hear them scold than fight. Exeunt Host, Shallow, and Page |
| FORD | Though Page be a secure [[archetypes/fool\|fool]] and stands so firmly on his wife’s frailty, yet I cannot put off my opinion so easily. She was in his company at Page’s house, and what they made there I know not. [[motifs/well\|Well]], I will look further into’t; and I have a disguise to sound [[characters/falstaff\|Falstaff]]. If I find her honest, I lose not my labour. If she be otherwise, ’tis labour [[motifs/well\|well]] bestowed. Exit |

### Atto 2, Scena 2

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Sir John [[characters/falstaff\|Falstaff]] and [[characters/pistol\|Pistol]] |
| SIR JOHN | I will not lend thee a penny. |
| [[characters/pistol\|PISTOL]] | I will retort the sum in equipage. |
| SIR JOHN | Not a penny. |
| *(didascalia)* | [[characters/pistol\|PISTOL]] drawing his sword Why then, the world’s mine oyster, which I with sword will open. |
| SIR JOHN | Not a penny. I have been content, sir, you should lay my countenance to pawn. I have grated upon my good friends for three reprieves for you and your coach-fellow Nim, or else you had looked through the grate like a gemini of baboons. I am damned in hell for swearing to gentlemen my friends you were good soldiers and tall fellows. And when Mistress Bridget lost the handle of her fan, I took’t upon mine [[concepts/honour\|honour]] thou hadst it not. |
| [[characters/pistol\|PISTOL]] | Didst not thou share? Hadst thou not fifteen pence? |
| SIR JOHN | Reason, you rogue, reason. Thinkest thou I’ll endanger my [[motifs/soul\|soul]] gratis? At a word, hang no more about me. I am no gibbet for you. Go, a short knife and a throng, to your manor of Pickt-hatch, go. You’ll not bear a letter for me, you rogue? You stand upon your [[concepts/honour\|honour]]? Why, thou unconfinable baseness, it is as much as I can do to keep the terms of my [[concepts/honour\|honour]] precise. Ay, ay, I myself sometimes, leaving the [[concepts/fear\|fear]] of God on the left hand, and hiding mine [[concepts/honour\|honour]] in my necessity, am fain to shuffle, to hedge, and to lurch; and yet you, you rogue, will ensconce your rags, your cat-a-mountain looks, your red-lattice phrases, and your bold beating oaths, under the shelter of your [[concepts/honour\|honour]]! You will not do it, you? |
| *(didascalia)* | [[characters/pistol\|PISTOL]] sheathing his sword |
| SIR JOHN | I do relent. What wouldst thou more of man? |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Robin |
| ROBIN | Sir, here’s a woman would speak with you. |
| SIR JOHN | Let her approach. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/mistress-quickly\|Mistress Quickly]] |
| [[characters/mistress-quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | Give your worship good morrow. |
| SIR JOHN | Good morrow, goodwife. |
| [[characters/mistress-quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | Not so, an’t please your worship. |
| SIR JOHN | Good maid, then. |
| [[characters/mistress-quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | I’ll be sworn: as my mother was the first hour I was born. |
| SIR JOHN | I do believe the swearer. What with me? |
| [[characters/mistress-quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | Shall I vouchsafe your worship a word or two? |
| SIR JOHN | Two thousand, fair woman, and I’ll vouchsafe thee the hearing. |
| [[characters/mistress-quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | There is one [[characters/mistress-ford\|Mistress Ford]], sir—I pray come a little nearer this ways.<br>She draws Sir John aside<br>I myself dwell with Master [[characters/doctor-caius\|Doctor Caius]]— |
| SIR JOHN | [[motifs/well\|Well]], on. [[characters/mistress-ford\|Mistress Ford]], ybu say. |
| [[characters/mistress-quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | Your worship says very true. I pray your worship come a little nearer this ways. |
| SIR JOHN | I warrant thee nobody hears. Mine own people, mine own people. |
| [[characters/mistress-quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | Are they so? God bless them and make them His servants! |
| SIR JOHN | [[motifs/well\|Well]], [[characters/mistress-ford\|Mistress Ford]]: what of her? |
| [[characters/mistress-quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | Why, sir, she’s a good creature. Lord, Lord, your worship’s a wanton! [[motifs/well\|Well]], heaven forgive you, and all of us, I pray— |
| SIR JOHN | [[characters/mistress-ford\|Mistress Ford]]; come, [[characters/mistress-ford\|Mistress Ford]]. |
| [[characters/mistress-quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | Marry, this is the short and the long of it. You have brought her into such a canaries as ’tis wonderful. The best courtier of them all, when the [[settings/court\|court]] lay at Windsor, could never have brought her to such a canary. Yet there has been knights, and lords, and gentlemen, with their coaches; I warrant you, coach after coach, letter after letter, gift after gift, smelling so sweetly, all musk; and so rustling, I warrant you, in silk and gold, and in such aligant terms, and in such wine and sugar of the best and the fairest, that would have won any woman’s heart; and, I warrant you, they could never get an eye-wink of her. I had myself twenty angels given me this morning—but I defy all angels, in any such sort, as they say, but in the way of honesty. And, I warrant you, they could never get her so much as sip on a cup with the proudest of them all. And yet there has been earls, nay, which is more, pensioners. But, I warrant you, all is one with her. |
| SIR JOHN | But what says she to me? Be brief, my good she-Mercury. |
| [[characters/mistress-quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | Marry, she hath received your letter, for the which she thanks you a thousand times, and she gives you to notify that her husband will be absence from his house between ten and eleven. |
| SIR JOHN | Ten and eleven. |
| [[characters/mistress-quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | Ay, forsooth, and then you may come and see the picture, she says, that you wot of. [[characters/master-ford\|Master Ford]], her husband, will be from [[motifs/home\|home]]. Alas, the sweet woman leads an ill life with him. He’s a very [[concepts/jealousy\|jealousy]] man. She leads a very frampold life with him, good heart. |
| SIR JOHN | Ten and eleven. Woman, commend me to her. I will not fail her. |
| [[characters/mistress-quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | Why, you say [[motifs/well\|well]]. But I have another messenger to your worship. [[characters/mistress-page\|Mistress Page]] hath her hearty commendations to you too; and, let me tell you in your ear, she’s as fartuous a civil modest wife, and one, I tell you, that will not miss you morning nor evening [[motifs/prayer\|prayer]], as any is in Windsor, whoe’er be the other; and she bade me tell your worship that her husband is seldom from [[motifs/home\|home]], but she hopes there will come a [[motifs/time\|time]]. I never knew a woman so dote upon a man. Surely I think you have charms, la; yes, in [[concepts/truth\|truth]]. |
| SIR JOHN | Not I, I assure thee. Setting the attraction of my good parts aside, I have no other charms. |
| [[characters/mistress-quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | Blessing on your heart for’t! |
| SIR JOHN | But I pray thee tell me this: has Ford’s wife and Page’s wife acquainted each other how they [[motifs/love\|love]] me? |
| [[characters/mistress-quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | O God no, sir; that were a jest indeed! They have not so little grace, I [[concepts/hope\|hope]]. That were a trick indeed! But [[characters/mistress-page\|Mistress Page]] would [[motifs/desire\|desire]] you to send her your little page of all loves. Her husband has a marvellous infection to the little page; and, truly, [[characters/master-page\|Master Page]] is an honest man. Never a wife in Windsor leads a better life than she does. Do what she will; say what she will; take all, pay all; go to bed when she list; rise when she list; all is as she will. And, truly, she deserves it, for if there be a kind woman in Windsor, she is one. You must send her your page, no remedy. |
| SIR JOHN | Why, I will. |
| [[characters/mistress-quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | Nay, but do so, then; and, look you, he may come and go between you both. And in any case have a nay-word, that you may know one another’s mind, and the boy never need to understand anything—for ’tis not good that children should know any wickedness. Old folks, you know, have discretion, as they say, and know the world. |
| SIR JOHN | Fare thee [[motifs/well\|well]]. Commend me to them both. There’s my purse; I am yet thy debtor.—Boy, go along with this woman. Exeunt [[characters/mistress-quickly\|Mistress Quickly]] and Robin (Aside) This news distracts me. |
| [[characters/pistol\|PISTOL]] | (aside)<br>This punk is one of Cupid’s carriers.<br>Clap on more sails! Pursue! Up with your sights!<br>Give [[motifs/fire\|fire]]! She is my prize, or ocean whelm them all! |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit |
| SIR JOHN | Sayst thou so, old Jack? Go thy ways! I’ll make more of thy old [[concepts/body\|body]] than I have done. Will they yet look after thee? Wilt thou, after the expense of so much money, be now a gainer? Good [[concepts/body\|body]], I thank thee. Let them say ’tis grossly done; so it be fairly done, no matter. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Bardolph, with sack |
| BARDOLPH | Sir John, there’s one Master Brooke below would fain speak with you and be acquainted with you, and hath sent your worship a morning’s draught of sack. |
| SIR JOHN | Brooke is his name? |
| BARDOLPH | Ay, sir. |
| *(didascalia)* | SIR JOHN Call him in. Drinking sack Such Brookes are welcome to me, that o’ehlows such liquor. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit Bardolph |
| BARDOLPH | Aha, [[characters/mistress-ford\|Mistress Ford]] and [[characters/mistress-page\|Mistress Page]], have I |
| *(didascalia)* | encompassed you? Drinking Go to. Via! |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Bardolph, and [[characters/master-ford\|Master Ford]] disguised as Brooke |
| FORD | God bless you, sir. |
| SIR JOHN | And you, sir. Would you speak with me? |
| FORD | I make bold to press with so little preparation upon you. |
| SIR JOHN | You’re welcome. What’s your will? (To Bardolph) Give us leave, drawer. Exit Bardolph |
| FORD | Sir, I am a gentleman that have spent much. My name is Brooke. |
| SIR JOHN | Good Master Brooke, I [[motifs/desire\|desire]] more acquaintance of you. |
| FORD | Good Sir John, I sue for yours—not to charge you, for I must let you understand I think myself in better plight for a lender than you are; the which hath something emboldened me to this unseasoned intrusion; for they say if money go before, all ways do lie open. |
| SIR JOHN | Money is a good soldier, sir, and will on. |
| FORD | Troth, and I have a bag of money here troubles me. If you will help to bear it, Sir John, take half, or all, for easing me of the carriage. |
| SIR JOHN | Sir, I know not how I may deserve to be your porter. |
| FORD | I will tell you, sir, if you will give me the hearing. |
| SIR JOHN | Speak, good Master Brooke. I shall be glad to be your servant. |
| FORD | Sir, I hear you are a scholar—I will be brief with you—and you have been a man long known to me, though I had never so good means as [[motifs/desire\|desire]] to make myself acquainted with you. I shall discover a thing to you wherein I must very much lay open mine own imperfection; but, good Sir John, as you have one eye upon my follies, as you hear them unfolded, turn another into the register of your own, that I may pass with a reproof the easier, sith you yourself know how easy it is to be such an offender. |
| SIR JOHN | Very [[motifs/well\|well]], sir, proceed. |
| FORD | There is a gentlewoman in this town; her husband’s name is Ford. |
| SIR JOHN | [[motifs/well\|Well]], sir. |
| FORD | I have long loved her, and, I protest to you, bestowed much on her, followed her with a doting observance, engrossed opportunities to meet her, fee’d every slight occasion that could but niggardly give me sight of her; not only bought many presents to give her, but have given largely to many to know what she would have given. Briefly, I have pursued her as [[motifs/love\|love]] hath pursued me, which hath been on the wing of all occasions. But, whatsoever I have merited, either in my mind or in my means, meed I am sure I have received none, unless experience be a [[motifs/jewel\|jewel]]. That I have purchased at an infinite rate, and that hath taught me to say this: ‘[[motifs/love\|Love]] like a [[archetypes/shadow\|shadow]] flies when substance [[motifs/love\|love]] pursues, Pursuing that that flies, and flying what pursues.’ |
| SIR JOHN | Have you received no promise of satisfaction at her hands? |
| FORD | Never. |
| SIR JOHN | Have you importuned her to such a purpose? |
| FORD | Never. |
| SIR JOHN | Of what quality was your [[motifs/love\|love]] then? |
| FORD | Like a fair house built on another man’s ground, so that I have lost my edifice by mistaking the place where I erected it. |
| SIR JOHN | To what purpose have you unfolded this to me? |
| FORD | When I have told you that, I have told you all. Some say that though she appear honest to me, yet in other places she enlargeth her mirth so far that there is shrewd construction made of her. Now, Sir John, here is the heart of my purpose. You are a gentleman of excellent breeding, admirable discourse, of great admittance, authentic in your place and person, generally allowed for your many warlike, [[settings/court\|court]]-like, and learned preparations. |
| SIR JOHN | O sir! |
| FORD | Believe it, for you know it. There is money. |
| *(didascalia)* | He offers money |
| FORD | Spend it, spend it; spend more; spend all I have; only give me so much of your [[motifs/time\|time]] in exchange of it as to lay an amiable siege to the honesty of this Ford’s wife. Use your art of wooing, win her to consent to you. If any man may, you may as soon as any. |
| SIR JOHN | Would it apply [[motifs/well\|well]] to the vehemency of your affection that I should win what you would enjoy? Methinks you prescribe to yourself very preposterously. |
| FORD | O, understand my drift. She dwells so securely on the excellency of her [[concepts/honour\|honour]] that the folly of my [[motifs/soul\|soul]] dares not present itself. She is too bright to be looked against. Now, could I come to her with any detection in my hand, my desires had instance and argument to commend themselves. I could drive her then from the ward of her purity, her reputation, her [[motifs/marriage\|marriage]] vow, and a thousand other her defences which now are too too strongly embattled against me. What say you to’t, Sir John? |
| SIR JOHN | Master Brooke, I will first make bold with your money. |
| *(didascalia)* | He takes the money |
| SIR JOHN | Next, give me your hand.<br>He takes his hand<br>And last, as I am a gentleman, you shall, if you will, enjoy Ford’s wife. |
| FORD | O, good sir! |
| SIR JOHN | I say you shall. |
| FORD | Want no money, Sir John, you shall want none. |
| SIR JOHN | Want no [[characters/mistress-ford\|Mistress Ford]], Master Brooke, you shall want none. I shall be with her, I may tell you, by her own appointment. Even as you came in to me, her spokesmate, or go-between, parted from me. I say I shall be with her between ten and eleven, for at that [[motifs/time\|time]] the jealous rascally knave her husband will be forth. Come you to me at [[motifs/night\|night]]; you shall know how I [[characters/speed\|speed]]. |
| FORD | I am blessed in your acquaintance. Do you know Ford, sir? |
| SIR JOHN | Hang him, poor cuckoldly knave, I know him not. Yet I wrong him to call him poor. They say the jealous wittolly knave hath masses of money, for the which his wife seems to me [[motifs/well\|well]] favoured. I will use her as the key of the cuckoldly rogue’s coffer, and there’s my harvest-[[motifs/home\|home]]. |
| FORD | I would you knew Ford, sir, that you might avoid him if you saw him. |
| SIR JOHN | Hang him, mechanical salt-butter rogue! I will stare him out of his wits. I will awe him with my cudgel; it shall hang like a meteor o’er the cuckold’s horns. Master Brooke, thou shalt know I will predominate over the peasant, and thou shalt lie with his wife. Come to me soon at [[motifs/night\|night]]. Ford’s a knave, and I will aggravate his style: thou, Master Brooke, shalt know him for knave and cuckold. Come to me soon at [[motifs/night\|night]]. Exit |
| FORD | What a damned epicurean rascal is this! My heart is ready to crack with impatience. Who says this is improvident [[concepts/jealousy\|jealousy]]? My wife hath sent to him, the hour is fixed, the match is made. Would any man have thought this? See the hell of having a false woman! My bed shall be abused, my coffers ransacked, my reputation gnawn at, and I shall not only receive this villainous wrong, but stand under the adoption of abominable terms, and by him that does me this wrong. Terms! Names! ‘Amaimon’ sounds [[motifs/well\|well]], ‘[[characters/lucifer\|Lucifer]]’ [[motifs/well\|well]], ‘Barbason’ [[motifs/well\|well]]; yet they are devils’ additions, the names of fiends. But ‘cuckold’, ‘wittot’! ’Cuckold‘—the devil himself hath not such a name. Page is an ass, a secure ass. He will trust his wife, he will not be jealous. I will rather trust a Fleming with my butter, Parson Hugh the Welshman with my cheese, an Irishman with my aqua-vitae bottle, or a thief to walk my ambling gelding, than my wife with herself. Then she plots, then she ruminates, then she devises; and what they think in their hearts they may effect, they will break their hearts but they will effect. God be praised for my [[concepts/jealousy\|jealousy]]! Eleven o’[[motifs/clock\|clock]] the hour. I will prevent this, detect my wife, be revenged on [[characters/falstaff\|Falstaff]], and laugh at Page. I will about it. Better three hours too soon than a minute too late. God’s my life: cuckold, cuckold, cuckold! Exit |

### Atto 2, Scena 3

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/doctor-caius\|Doctor Caius]] and John Rugby, with rapiers CAIUS Jack Rugby! |
| RUGBY | Sir. |
| CAIUS | Vat is the [[motifs/clock\|clock]], Jack? |
| RUGBY | ’Tis past the hour, sir, that Sir Hugh promised to meet. |
| CAIUS | By Gar, he has save his [[motifs/soul\|soul]] dat he is no come; he has pray his Pible [[motifs/well\|well]] dat he is no come. By Gar, Jack Rugby, he is dead already if he be come. |
| RUGBY | He is wise, sir, he knew your worship would kill him if he came. |
| *(didascalia)* | CAIUS drawing his rapier By Gar, de herring is no dead so as I vill kill him. Take your rapier, Jack. I vill tell you how I vill kill him. |
| RUGBY | Alas, sir, I cannot fence. |
| CAIUS | Villainy, take your rapier. |
| RUGBY | Forbear: here’s company. |
| *(didascalia)* | Caius sheathes his rapier. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the Host of the Garter, [[characters/justice-shallow\|Justice Shallow]], |
| RUGBY | [[characters/master-page\|Master Page]], and Master Slender |
| HOST | God bless thee, bully Doctor. |
| SHALLOW | God save you, Master [[characters/doctor-caius\|Doctor Caius]]. |
| PAGE | Now, good Master Doctor. |
| SLENDER | Give you good morrow, sir. |
| CAIUS | Vat be all you, one, two, tree, four, come for? |
| HOST | To see thee fight, to see thee foin, to see thee traverse, to see thee here, to see thee there; to see thee pass thy punto, thy stock, thy reverse, thy distance, thy montant. Is he dead, my Ethiopian? Is he dead, my Francisco? Ha, bully? What says my Aesculapius, my Galen, my heart of elder, ha? Is he dead, bully stale? Is he dead? |
| CAIUS | By Gar, he is de coward jack-priest of de vorld. He is not show his face. |
| HOST | Thou art a Castalian [[archetypes/king\|King]] Urinal, [[characters/hector\|Hector]] of Greece, my boy. |
| CAIUS | I pray you bear witness that me have stay six or seven, two, tree hours for him, and he is no come. |
| SHALLOW | He is the wiser man, Master Doctor. He is a curer of souls, and you a curer of bodies. If you should fight you go against the hair of your professions. Is it not true, [[characters/master-page\|Master Page]]? |
| PAGE | Master Shallow, you have yourself been a great fighter, though now a man of peace. |
| SHALLOW | Bodykins, [[characters/master-page\|Master Page]], though I now be old and of the peace, if I see a sword out my finger itches to make one. Though we are justices and doctors and churchmen, [[characters/master-page\|Master Page]], we have some salt of our youth in us. We are the sons of women, [[characters/master-page\|Master Page]]. |
| PAGE | ’Tis true, Master Shallow. |
| SHALLOW | It will be found so, [[characters/master-page\|Master Page]].—Master [[characters/doctor-caius\|Doctor Caius]], I am come to fetch you [[motifs/home\|home]]. I am sworn of the peace. You have showed yourself a wise physician, and Sir Hugh hath shown himself a wise and patient churchman. You must go with me, Master Doctor. |
| HOST | Pardon, guest Justice. (To Caius) A word, Monsieur Mockwater. |
| CAIUS | Mockvater? Vat is dat? |
| HOST | Mockwater, in our English tongue, is valour, bully. |
| CAIUS | By Gar, then I have as much mockvater as de Englishman. Scurvy jack-dog priest! By Gar, me vill cut his ears. |
| HOST | He will clapper-claw thee tightly, bully. |
| CAIUS | Clapper-de-claw? Vat is dat? |
| HOST | That is, he will make thee amends. |
| CAIUS | By Gar, me do look he shall clapper-de-claw me, for, by Gar, me vill have it. |
| HOST | And I will provoke him to’t, or let him wag. |
| CAIUS | Me tank you for dat. |
| HOST | And moreover, bully—(Aside to the others) But first, master guest and [[characters/master-page\|Master Page]], and eke Cavaliero Slender, go you through the town to Frogmore. |
| PAGE | Sir Hugh is there, is he? |
| HOST | He is there. See what humour he is in, and I will bring the Doctor about by the fields. Will it do [[motifs/well\|well]]? |
| SHALLOW | We will do it. |
| *(didascalia)* | PAGE, SHALLOW, and SLENDER Adieu, good Master Doctor. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt Page, Shallow, and Slender |
| *(didascalia)* | CAIUS drawing his rapier By Gar, me vill kill de priest, for he speak for a jackanape to [[characters/anne-page\|Anne Page]]. |
| HOST | Let him die. Sheathe thy impatience; throw cold [[motifs/water\|water]] on thy choler. Go about the fields with me through Frogmore. I will bring thee where Mistress [[characters/anne-page\|Anne Page]] is, at a farmhouse a-feasting; and thou shalt woo her. Cried game? Said I [[motifs/well\|well]]? |
| *(didascalia)* | CAIUS sheathing his rapier By Gar, me dank you vor dat. By Gar, I [[motifs/love\|love]] you, and I shall procure-a you de good guest: de earl, de knight, de lords, de gentlemen, my patiences. |
| HOST | For the which I will be thy adversary toward [[characters/anne-page\|Anne Page]]. Said I [[motifs/well\|well]]? |
| CAIUS | By Gar, ’tis good. Vell said. |
| HOST | Let us wag, then. |
| CAIUS | Come at my heels, Jack Rugby. Exeunt |

### Atto 3, Scena 1

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Sir Hugh Evans with a rapier, and bearing a book and Simple bearing Evans’s gown |
| EVANS | I pray you now, good Master Slender’s servingman, and friend Simple by your name, which way have you looked for Master Caius, that calls himself Doctor of Physic? |
| SIMPLE | Marry, sir, the Petty Ward, the Park Ward, every way; old Windsor way, and every way but the town way. |
| EVANS | I most fehemently [[motifs/desire\|desire]] you you will also look that way. |
| *(didascalia)* | SIMPLE I will, sir. Exit |
| *(didascalia)* | EVANS opening the book Jeshu pless me, how full of cholers I am, and trempling of mind! I shall be glad if he have deceived me. How melancholies I am! I will knog his urinals about his knave’s [[characters/costard\|costard]] when I have good opportunities for the ’ork. Pless my [[motifs/soul\|soul]]!—(Singing) |
| EVANS | To shallow rivers, to whose falls<br>Melodious [[motifs/birds\|birds]] sings madrigals.<br>There will we make our peds of roses,<br>And a thousand fragrant posies.<br>To shallow—<br>[[concepts/mercy\|Mercy]] on me! I have a great dispositions to cry.—(Singing)<br>Melodious [[motifs/birds\|birds]] sing madrigals.—<br>When as I sat in Pabylon—<br>And a thousand vagram posies.<br>To shallow (etc.) |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Simple |
| SIMPLE | Yonder he is coming. This way, Sir Hugh. |
| EVANS | He’s welcome.<br>(Singing) ‘To shallow rivers to whose falls-’<br>God prosper the right! What weapons is he? |
| SIMPLE | No weapons, sir. There comes my master, Master<br>Shallow, and another gentleman, from Frogmore, over<br>the stile this way. |
| EVANS | Pray you give me my gown—or else keep it in your arms. |
| *(didascalia)* | He reads. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/justice-shallow\|Justice Shallow]], Master Slender, and Master |
| EVANS | Page |
| SHALLOW | How now, Master Parson? Good morrow, good Sir Hugh. Keep a gamester from the dice and a good student from his book, and it is wonderful. |
| SLENDER | (aside) Ah, sweet [[characters/anne-page\|Anne Page]]! |
| PAGE | God save you, good Sir Hugh. |
| EVANS | God pless you from his [[concepts/mercy\|mercy]] sake, all of you. |
| SHALLOW | What, the sword and the Word? Do you study them both, Master Parson? |
| PAGE | And youthful still: in your doublet and hose this raw, rheumatic day I |
| EVANS | There is reasons and causes for it. |
| PAGE | We are come to you to do a good office, Master Parson. |
| EVANS | Fery [[motifs/well\|well]]. What is it? |
| PAGE | Yonder is a most reverend gentleman, who, belike having received wrong by some person, is at most odds with his own gravity and patience that ever you saw. |
| SHALLOW | I have lived fourscore years and upward; I never heard a man of his place, gravity, and learning so wide of his own respect. |
| EVANS | What is he? |
| PAGE | I think you know him: Master [[characters/doctor-caius\|Doctor Caius]], the renowned French physician. |
| EVANS | Got’s will and his passion of my heart! I had as lief you would tell me of a mess of pottage. |
| PAGE | Why? |
| EVANS | He has no more knowledge in Hibbocrates and Galen, and he is a knave besides—a cowardly knave as you would desires to be acquainted withal. |
| *(didascalia)* | PAGE to Shallow I warrant you, he’s the man should fight with him. |
| SLENDER | (aside) O sweet [[characters/anne-page\|Anne Page]]! |
| SHALLOW | It appears so by his weapons. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the Host of the Garter, [[characters/doctor-caius\|Doctor Caius]], and John Rugby |
| SHALLOW | Keep them asunder—here comes [[characters/doctor-caius\|Doctor Caius]].<br>Evans and Caius draw and offer to fight |
| PAGE | Nay, good Master Parson, keep in your weapon. |
| SHALLOW | So do you, good Master Doctor. |
| HOST | Disarm them and let them question. Let them keep their limbs whole, and hack our English.<br>Shallow and Page take Caius’s and Evans’s rapiers |
| CAIUS | (to Evans) I pray you let-a me speak a word with your ear. Wherefore vill you not meet-a me? |
| *(didascalia)* | EVANS aside to Caius Pray you use your patience. Aloud In good [[motifs/time\|time]]! |
| CAIUS | By Gar, you are de coward, de jack-dog, john-ape. |
| EVANS | (aside to Caius) Pray you let us not be laughing-stocks to other men’s humours. I [[motifs/desire\|desire]] you in [[concepts/friendship\|friendship]], and I will one way or other make you amends. (Aloud) By Jeshu, I will knog your urinal about your knave’s cogscomb. |
| CAIUS | Diable! Jack Rugby, mine Host de Jarteer, have I not stay for him to kill him? Have I not, at de place I did appoint? |
| EVANS | As I am a Christians [[motifs/soul\|soul]], now look you, this is the place appointed. I’ll be judgement by mine Host of the Garter. |
| HOST | Peace, I say, Gallia and Gaul, French and Welsh, [[motifs/soul\|soul]]-curer and [[concepts/body\|body]]-curer. |
| CAIUS | Ay, dat is very good, excellent. |
| HOST | Peace, I say. Hear mine Host of the Garter. Am I politic? Am I subtle? Am I a Machiavel? Shall I lose my doctor? No, he gives me the potions and the motions. Shall I lose my parson, my priest, my Sir Hugh? No, he gives me the Proverbs and the No-verbs. (To Caius) Give me thy hand terrestrial—so. (To Evans) Give me thy hand celestial—so. Boys of art, I have deceived you both, I have directed you to wrong places. Your hearts are mighty, your skins are whole, and let burnt sack be the issue. (To Shallow and Page) Come, lay their swords to pawn. (To Caius and Evans) Follow me, lads of peace, follow, follow, follow. Exit |
| SHALLOW | Afore God, a mad host! Follow, gentlemen, follow. Exeunt Shallow and Page |
| SLENDER | (aside) O sweet [[characters/anne-page\|Anne Page]] Exit |
| CAIUS | Ha, do I perceive dat? Have you make-a de sot of us, ha, ha? |
| EVANS | This is [[motifs/well\|well]]: he has made us his vlouting-stog. I [[motifs/desire\|desire]] you that we may be friends, and let us knog our prains together to be [[concepts/revenge\|revenge]] on this same scall, scurvy, cogging companion, the Host of the Garter. |
| CAIUS | By Gar, with all my heart. He promise to bring me where is [[characters/anne-page\|Anne Page]]. By Gar, he deceive me too. |
| EVANS | [[motifs/well\|Well]], I will smite his noddles. Pray you follow. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt |

### Atto 3, Scena 2

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Robin, followed by [[characters/mistress-page\|Mistress Page]] |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | Nay, keep your way, little gallant. You were wont to be a follower, but now you are a leader. Whether had you rather, lead mine eyes, or eye your master’s heels? |
| ROBIN | I had rather, forsooth, go before you like a man than follow him like a [[characters/dwarf\|dwarf]]. |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | O, you are a flattering boy! Now I see you’ll be a courtier. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/master-ford\|Master Ford]] |
| FORD | [[motifs/well\|Well]] met, [[characters/mistress-page\|Mistress Page]]. Whither go you? |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | Truly, sir, to see your wife. Is she at [[motifs/home\|home]]? |
| FORD | Ay, and as idle as she may hang together, for want of company. I think if your husbands were dead you two would marry. |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | Be sure of that—two other husbands. |
| FORD | Where had you this pretty weathercock? |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | I cannot tell what the dickens his name is my husband had him of.—What do you call your knight’s name, sirrah? |
| ROBIN | Sir John [[characters/falstaff\|Falstaff]]. |
| FORD | Sir John [[characters/falstaff\|Falstaff]]? |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | He, he; I can never hit on’s name. There is such a league between my goodman and he! Is your wife at [[motifs/home\|home]] indeed? |
| FORD | Indeed she is. |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | By your leave, sir, I am sick till I see her. Exeunt Robin and Mistress |
| FORD | Has Page any brains? Hath he any eyes? Hath he any thinking? Sure they [[motifs/sleep\|sleep]]; he hath no use of them. Why, this boy will carry a letter twenty mile, as easy as a cannon will shoot point-blank twelve score. He pieces out his wife’s inclination; he gives her folly motion and advantage. And now she’s going to my wife, and [[characters/falstaff\|Falstaff]]’s boy with her. A man may hear this shower sing in the [[motifs/wind\|wind]]. And [[characters/falstaff\|Falstaff]]’s boy with her. Good plots—they are laid; and our revolted wives share damnation together. [[motifs/well\|Well]], I will take him; then torture my wife, pluck the borrowed veil of modesty from the so-seeming [[characters/mistress-page\|Mistress Page]], divulge Page himself for a secure and wilful Actaeon, and to these violent proceedings all my neighbours shall cry aim. |
| *(didascalia)* | [[motifs/clock\|Clock]] strikes |
| FORD | The [[motifs/clock\|clock]] gives me my cue, and my assurance bids me search. There I shall find [[characters/falstaff\|Falstaff]]. I shall be rather praised for this than mocked, for it is as positive as the earth is firm that [[characters/falstaff\|Falstaff]] is there. I will go. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/master-page\|Master Page]], [[characters/justice-shallow\|Justice Shallow]], Master Slender, the Host of the Garter, Sir Hugh Evans, [[characters/doctor-caius\|Doctor Caius]], and John Rugby |
| FORD | SHALLOW, PAGE, etc. [[motifs/well\|Well]] met, [[characters/master-ford\|Master Ford]]. |
| FORD | (aside) By my [[concepts/faith\|faith]], a good knot! (To them) I have good cheer at [[motifs/home\|home]], and I pray you all go with me. |
| SHALLOW | I must excuse myself, [[characters/master-ford\|Master Ford]]. |
| SLENDER | And so must I, sir. We have appointed to dine with Mistress Anne, and I would not break with her for more money than I’ll speak of. |
| SHALLOW | We have lingered about a match between [[characters/anne-page\|Anne Page]] and my cousin Slender, and this day we shall have our answer. |
| SLENDER | I [[concepts/hope\|hope]] I have your good will, father Page. |
| PAGE | You have, Master Slender: I stand wholly for you. (To Caius) But my wife, Master Doctor, is for you altogether. |
| CAIUS | Ay, be Gar, and de maid is [[motifs/love\|love]]-a me. My nursh-a Quickly tell me so mush. |
| HOST | (to Page) What say you to young Master [[characters/fenton\|Fenton]]? He capers, he dances, he has eyes of youth; he writes verses, he speaks holiday, he smells April and May. He will carry‘t, he will carry’t; ’tis in his buttons he will carry’t. |
| PAGE | Not by my consent, I promise you. The gentleman is of no having. He kept company with the wild Prince and Poins. He is of too high a region; he knows too much. No, he shall not knit a knot in his fortunes with the finger of my substance. If he take her, let him take her simply: the wealth I have waits on my consent, and my consent goes not that way. |
| FORD | I beseech you heartily, some of you go [[motifs/home\|home]] with me to dinner. Besides your cheer, you shall have sport: I will show you a [[archetypes/monster\|monster]]. Master Doctor, you shall go. So shall you, [[characters/master-page\|Master Page]], and you, Sir Hugh. |
| *(didascalia)* | SHALLOW [[motifs/well\|Well]], God be with you! Aside to Slender We shall have the freer wooing at [[characters/master-page\|Master Page]]’s. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt Shallow and Slender |
| CAIUS | Go [[motifs/home\|home]], John Rugby; I come anon. Exit Rugby HOST Farewell, my hearts. I will to my honest knight [[characters/falstaff\|Falstaff]], and drink canary with him. Exit |
| FORD | (aside) I think I shall drink in pipe-wine first with him: I’ll make him [[motifs/dance\|dance]]. (To Page, Caius, and Evans) Will you go, gentles? |
| *(didascalia)* | PAGE, CAIUS, and EVANS Have with you to see this [[archetypes/monster\|monster]]. Exeunt |

### Atto 3, Scena 3

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/mistress-ford\|Mistress Ford]] and [[characters/mistress-page\|Mistress Page]] |
| [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] | What, John! What, Robert! |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | Quickly, quickly! Is the buck-basket- |
| [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] | I warrant.—What, Robert, I say! |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | Come, come, come! |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter John and Robert, with a buck-basket |
| [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] | Here, set it down. |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | Give your men the charge. We must be brief. |
| [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] | Marry, as I told you before, John and Robert, be ready here hard by in the brew-house; and when I suddenly call you, come forth, and without any pause or staggering take this basket on your shoulders. That done, trudge with it in all haste, and carry it among the whitsters in Datchet Mead, and there empty it in the muddy ditch close by the Thames’ side. |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | (to John and Robert) You will do it? |
| [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] | I ha’ told them over and over; they lack no direction.—Be gone, and come when you are called. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt John and Robert |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Robin |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | Here comes little Robin. |
| [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] | How now, my eyas-musket, what news with you? |
| ROBIN | My master Sir John is come in at your back door, [[characters/mistress-ford\|Mistress Ford]], and requests your company. |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | You little Jack-a-Lent, have you been true to us? |
| ROBIN | Ay, I’ll be sworn. My master knows not of your being here, and hath threatened to put me into everlasting liberty if I tell you of it; for he swears he’ll turn me away. |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | Thou’rt a good boy. This secrecy of thine shall be a tailor to thee, and shall make thee a new doublet and hose.—I’ll go hide me. |
| [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] | Do so. (To Robin) Go tell thy master I am alone. Exit Robin [[characters/mistress-page\|Mistress Page]], remember you your cue. |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | I warrant thee. If I do not act it, hiss me. |
| *(didascalia)* | [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] Go to, then. Exit [[characters/mistress-page\|Mistress Page]] |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | We’ll use this unwholesome humidity, this gross watery<br>pumpkin. We’ll teach him to know turtles from jays. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Sir John [[characters/falstaff\|Falstaff]] |
| SIR JOHN | Have I caught thee, my heavenly [[motifs/jewel\|jewel]]? Why, now let me die, for I have lived long enough. This is the period of my [[concepts/ambition\|ambition]]. O, this blessed hour! |
| [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] | O sweet Sir John! |
| SIR JOHN | [[characters/mistress-ford\|Mistress Ford]], I cannot cog; I cannot prate, [[characters/mistress-ford\|Mistress Ford]]. Now shall I sin in my wish: I would thy husband were dead. I’ll speak it before the best lord. I would make thee my lady. |
| [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] | I your lady, Sir John? Alas, I should be a pitiful lady. |
| SIR JOHN | Let the [[settings/court\|court]] of France show me such another. I see how thine eye would emulate the diamond. Thou hast the right arched [[concepts/beauty\|beauty]] of the brow that becomes the [[motifs/ship\|ship]]-tire, the tire-valiant, or any tire of Venetian admittance. |
| [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] | A plain kerchief, Sir John—my brows become nothing else, nor that [[motifs/well\|well]] neither. |
| SIR JOHN | By the Lord, thou art a [[archetypes/tyrant\|tyrant]] to say so. Thou wouldst make an absolute courtier, and the firm fixture of thy foot would give an excellent motion to thy gait in a semicircled farthingale. I see what thou wert if fortune, thy foe, were, with [[concepts/nature\|nature]], thy friend. Come, thou canst not hide it. |
| [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] | Believe me, there’s no such thing in me. SIR JOHN What made me [[motifs/love\|love]] thee? Let that persuade thee there’s something extraordinary in thee. Come, I cannot cog and say thou art this and that, like a-many of these lisping hawthorn-buds that come like women in men’s apparel and smell like Bucklersbury in simple [[motifs/time\|time]]; I cannot. But I [[motifs/love\|love]] thee, none but thee; and thou deservest it. |
| [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] | Do not betray me, sir. I [[concepts/fear\|fear]] you [[motifs/love\|love]] [[characters/mistress-page\|Mistress Page]]. |
| SIR JOHN | Thou mightst as [[motifs/well\|well]] say I [[motifs/love\|love]] to walk by the Counter gate, which is as hateful to me as the reek of a lime-kiln. |
| [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] | [[motifs/well\|Well]], heaven knows how I [[motifs/love\|love]] you; and you shall one day find it. |
| SIR JOHN | Keep in that mind. I’ll deserve it. |
| [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] | Nay, I must tell you, so you do; or else I could not be in that mind. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Robin |
| ROBIN | [[characters/mistress-ford\|Mistress Ford]], [[characters/mistress-ford\|Mistress Ford]]! Here’s [[characters/mistress-page\|Mistress Page]] at the door, sweating and blowing, and looking wildly, and would needs speak with you presently. |
| SIR JOHN | She shall not see me. I will ensconce me behind the arras. |
| [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] | Pray you do so; she’s a very tattling woman.<br>Sir John hides behind the arras. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/mistress-page\|Mistress Page]] |
| [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] | What’s the matter? How now? |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | O [[characters/mistress-ford\|Mistress Ford]], what have you done? You’re shamed, you’re overthrown, you’re undone for ever. |
| [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] | What’s the matter, good [[characters/mistress-page\|Mistress Page]]? |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | O [[motifs/well\|well]]-a-day, [[characters/mistress-ford\|Mistress Ford]]! Having an honest man to your husband, to give him such cause of suspicion! |
| [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] | What cause of suspicion? |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | What cause of suspicion? Out upon you!<br>How am I mistook in you |
| [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] | Why, alas, what’s the matter? |
