| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | Your husband’s coming hither, woman, with all the officers in Windsor, to search for a gentleman that he says is here now in the house, by your consent, to take an ill advantage of his absence. You are undone. |
| [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] | ’Tis not so, I [[concepts/hope\|hope]]. |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | Pray heaven it be not so that you have such a man here! But ’tis most certain your husband’s coming, with half Windsor at his heels, to search for such a one. I come before to tell you. If you know yourself clear, why, I am glad of it; but if you have a friend here, convey, convey him out. Be not amazed. Call all your senses to you. Defend your reputation, or bid farewell to your good life for ever. |
| [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] | What shall I do? There is a gentleman, my dear friend; and I [[concepts/fear\|fear]] not mine own shame so much as his peril. I had rather than a thousand pound he were out of the house. |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | For shame, never stand ‘you had rather’ and ‘you had rather’. Your husband’s here at hand. Bethink you of some conveyance: in the house you cannot hide him. O, how have you deceived me! Look, here is a basket. If he be of any reasonable stature, he may creep in here; and throw foul linen upon him as if it were going to bucking. Or—it is whiting [[motifs/time\|time]]—send him by your two men to Datchet Mead. |
| [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] | He’s too big to go in there. What shall I do? |
| SIR JOHN | (coming forward) Let me see’t, let me see’t, O let me see’t! I’ll in, I’ll in. Follow your friend’s counsel; I’ll in. |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | What, Sir John [[characters/falstaff\|Falstaff]]! (Aside to him) Are these your letters, knight? |
| SIR JOHN | (aside to [[characters/mistress-page\|Mistress Page]]) I [[motifs/love\|love]] thee. Help me away. Let me creep in here.<br>He goes into the basket<br>I’ll never—<br>[[characters/mistress-page\|Mistress Page]] and [[characters/mistress-ford\|Mistress Ford]] put foul clothes over him |
| *(didascalia)* | [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] (to Robin) Help to cover your master, boy.—Call your men, [[characters/mistress-ford\|Mistress Ford]]. Aside to Sir John You dissembling knight! |
| [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] | What, John! Robert, John! |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter John and Robert |
| [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] | Go take up these clothes here quickly. Where’s the cowl-staff?<br>John and Robert fit the cowl-staff<br>Look how you drumble! Carry them to the laundress in Datchet Mead. Quickly, come!<br>They lift the basket and start to leave. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/master-ford\|Master Ford]], [[characters/master-page\|Master Page]], [[characters/doctor-caius\|Doctor Caius]], and |
| [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] | Sir Hugh Evans |
| FORD | (to Page, Caius, and Evans) Pray you come near. If I suspect without cause, why then, make sport at me; then let me be your jest—1 deserve it. (To John and Robert) How now? Whither bear you this? |
| *(didascalia)* | JOHN To the laundress, forsooth. |
| [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] | Why, what have you to do whither they bear it? You were best meddle with buck-washing! |
| FORD | Buck? I would I could wash myself of the buck! Buck, buck, buck? Ay, buck, I warrant you, buck. And of the season too, it shall appear. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt John and Robert, with the basket |
| FORD | Gentlemen, I have dreamt tonight. I’ll tell you my dream. Here, here, here be my keys. Ascend my chambers, search, seek, find out. I’ll warrant we’ll unkennel the fox. Let me stop this way first.<br>He locks the door<br>So, now, uncoop. |
| PAGE | Good [[characters/master-ford\|Master Ford]], be contented. You wrong yourself too much. |
| FORD | True, [[characters/master-page\|Master Page]].—Up, gentlemen! You shall see sport anon. Follow me, gentlemen. Exit |
| EVANS | This is fery fantastical humours and jealousies. |
| CAIDS | By Gar, ’tis no the fashion of France; it is not jealous in France. |
| PAGE | Nay, follow him, gentlemen. See the issue of his search. Exeunt Caius, Evans, and Page |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | Is there not a [[topoi/double\|double]] excellency in this? |
| [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] | I know not which pleases me better: that my husband is deceived, or Sir John. |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | What a taking was he in when your husband asked what was in the basket! |
| [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] | I am half afraid he will have need of washing, so throwing him into the [[motifs/water\|water]] will do him a benefit. |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | Hang him, dishonest rascal! I would all of the same strain were in the same distress. |
| [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] | I think my husband hath some special suspicion of [[characters/falstaff\|Falstaff]]’s being here, for I never saw him so gross in his [[concepts/jealousy\|jealousy]] till now. |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | I will lay a plot to try that, and we will yet have more tricks with [[characters/falstaff\|Falstaff]]. His dissolute disease will scarce obey this medicine. |
| [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] | Shall we send that foolish carrion [[characters/mistress-quickly\|Mistress Quickly]] to him, and excuse his throwing into the [[motifs/water\|water]], and give him another [[concepts/hope\|hope]], to betray him to another punishment? |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | We will do it. Let him be sent for tomorrow eight o’[[motifs/clock\|clock]], to have amends. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Ford, Page, Caius, and Evans |
| FORD | I cannot find him. Maybe the knave bragged of that he could not compass. |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | (aside to [[characters/mistress-ford\|Mistress Ford]]) Heard you that? |
| [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] | You use me [[motifs/well\|well]], [[characters/master-ford\|Master Ford]], do you? |
| FORD | Ay, I do so. |
| [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] | Heaven make me better than your thoughts! |
| FORD | Amen. |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | You do yourself mighty wrong, [[characters/master-ford\|Master Ford]]. |
| FORD | Ay, ay, I must bear it. |
| EVANS | If there be anypody in the house, and in the chambers, and in the coffers, and in the presses, heaven forgive my sins at the day of judgement! |
| CAIUS | Be Gar, nor I too. There is nobodies. |
| PAGE | Fie, fie, [[characters/master-ford\|Master Ford]], are you not ashamed? What spirit, what devil suggests this [[concepts/imagination\|imagination]]? I would not ha’ your distemper in this kind for the wealth of Windsor [[settings/castle\|Castle]]. |
| FORD | ’Tis my fault, [[characters/master-page\|Master Page]]. I suffer for it. |
| EVANS | You suffer for a pad [[concepts/conscience\|conscience]]. Your wife is as honest a ’omans as I will desires among five thousand, and five hundred too. |
| CAIUS | By Gar, I see ’tis an honest woman. |
| FORD | [[motifs/well\|Well]], I promised you a dinner. Come, come, walk in the park. I pray you pardon me. I will hereafter make known to you why I have done this.—Come, wife; come, [[characters/mistress-page\|Mistress Page]]. I pray you pardon me. Pray heartily pardon me. |
| PAGE | (to Caius and Evans) Let’s go in, gentlemen. (Aside to them) But trust me, we’ll mock him. (To Ford, Caius, and Evans) I do invite you tomorrow morning to my house to breakfast. After, we’ll a-birding together. I have a fine hawk for the bush. Shall it be so? |
| FORD | Anything. |
| EVANS | If there is one, I shall make two in the company. |
| CAIUS | If there be one or two, I shall make-a the turd. |
| FORD | Pray you go, [[characters/master-page\|Master Page]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt fall but Evans and Caius |
| EVANS | I pray you now, remembrance tomorrow on the lousy knave mine Host. |
| CAIUS | Dat is good, by Gar; with all my heart. |
| EVANS | A lousy knave, to have his gibes and his mockeries. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt |

### Atto 3, Scena 4

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Master [[characters/fenton\|Fenton]] and [[characters/anne-page\|Anne Page]] |
| [[characters/fenton\|FENTON]] | I see I cannot get thy father’s [[motifs/love\|love]];<br>Therefore no more turn me to him, sweet Nan. |
| ANNE | Alas, how then? |
| [[characters/fenton\|FENTON]] | Why, thou must be thyself.<br>He doth object I am too great of birth,<br>And that, my state being galled with my expense,<br>I seek to heal it only by his wealth.<br>Besides these, other bars he lays before me—<br>My riots past, my wild societies;<br>And tells me ’tis a thing impossible<br>I should [[motifs/love\|love]] thee but as a property. |
| ANNE | Maybe he tells you true. |
| *(didascalia)* | [[characters/fenton\|FENTON]] |
| ANNE | No, heaven so [[characters/speed\|speed]] me in my [[motifs/time\|time]] to come!<br>Albeit I will confess thy father’s wealth<br>Was the first motive that I wooed thee, Anne,<br>Yet, wooing thee, I found thee of more value<br>Than stamps in gold or sums in sealèd bags;<br>And ’tis the very riches of thyself<br>That now I aim at. |
| ANNE | Gentle Master [[characters/fenton\|Fenton]],<br>Yet seek my father’s [[motifs/love\|love]], still seek it, sir.<br>If opportunity and humblest suit<br>Cannot attain it, why then— |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/justice-shallow\|Justice Shallow]], Master Slender richly dressedl, and [[characters/mistress-quickly\|Mistress Quickly]] |
| ANNE | Hark you hither.<br>They talk apart |
| SHALLOW | Break their talk, [[characters/mistress-quickly\|Mistress Quickly]]. My kinsman shall speak for himself. |
| SLENDER | I’ll make a shaft or a bolt on’t. ’Slid, ’tis but venturing. |
| SHALLOW | Be not dismayed. |
| SLENDER | No, she shall not dismay me.<br>I care not for that, but that I am afeard. |
| [[characters/mistress-quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | (to Anne) Hark ye, Master Slender would speak a word with you. |
| ANNE | I come to him. (To [[characters/fenton\|Fenton]]) This is my father’s choice.<br>O, what a world of vile ill-favoured faults<br>Looks handsome in three hundred pounds a year I |
| [[characters/mistress-quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | And how does good Master [[characters/fenton\|Fenton]]?<br>Pray you, a word with you.<br>She draws [[characters/fenton\|Fenton]] aside |
| SHALLOW | She’s coming. To her, coz! O boy, thou hadst a father! |
| SLENDER | I had a father, Mistress Anne; my uncle can tell you good jests of him.—Pray you, uncle, tell Mistress Anne the jest how my father stole two geese out of a pen, good uncle. |
| SHALLOW | Mistress Anne, my cousin loves you. |
| SLENDER | Ay, that I do, as [[motifs/well\|well]] as I [[motifs/love\|love]] any woman in Gloucestershire. |
| SHALLOW | He will maintain you like a gentlewoman. |
| SLENDER | Ay, by God, that I will, come cut and long-tail, under the degree of a squire. |
| SHALLOW | He will make you a hundred and fifty pounds jointure. |
| ANNE | Good Master Shallow, let him woo for himself. |
| SHALLOW | Marry, I thank you for it, I thank you for that good comfort.—She calls you, coz. I’ll leave you.<br>He stands aside |
| ANNE | Now, Master Slender. |
| SLENDER | Now, good Mistress Anne. |
| ANNE | What is your will? |
| SLENDER | My will? ‘Od’s heartlings, that’s a pretty jest indeed! I ne’er made my will yet, I thank God; I am not such a sickly creature, I give God praise. |
| ANNE | I mean, Master Slender, what would you with me? |
| SLENDER | Truly, for mine own part, I would little or nothing with you. Your father and my uncle hath made motions. If it be my luck, so. If not, happy man be his dole. They can tell you how things go better than I can. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/master-page\|Master Page]] and [[characters/mistress-page\|Mistress Page]] |
| SLENDER | You may ask your father: here he comes. |
| PAGE | Now, Master Stender.—[[motifs/love\|Love]] him, daughter Anne.—<br>Why, how now? What does Master [[characters/fenton\|Fenton]] here?<br>You wrong me, sir, thus still to haunt my house.<br>I told you, sir, my daughter is disposed of. |
| [[characters/fenton\|FENTON]] | Nay, [[characters/master-page\|Master Page]], be not impatient. |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | Good Master [[characters/fenton\|Fenton]], come not to my [[archetypes/child\|child]]. |
| PAGE | She is no match for you. |
| [[characters/fenton\|FENTON]] | Sir, will you hear me? |
| PAGE | No, good Master Penton.—<br>Come, Master Shallow; come, son Slender, in.—<br>Knowing my mind, you wrong me, Master [[characters/fenton\|Fenton]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt Page, Shallow, and Slender |
| [[characters/mistress-quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | (to [[characters/fenton\|Fenton]]) Speak to [[characters/mistress-page\|Mistress Page]]. |
| [[characters/fenton\|FENTON]] | Good [[characters/mistress-page\|Mistress Page]], for that I [[motifs/love\|love]] your daughter<br>In such a righteous fashion as I do,<br>Perforce against all checks, rebukes, and manners<br>I must advance the colours of my [[motifs/love\|love]],<br>And not retire. Let me have your good will. |
| ANNE | Good mother, do not marry me to yon [[archetypes/fool\|fool]]. |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | I mean it not; I seek you a better husband. |
| *(didascalia)* | [[characters/mistress-quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] aside to Anne That’s my master, Master Doctor. |
| ANNE | Alas, I had rather be set quick i’th’ earth<br>And bowled to [[motifs/death\|death]] with turnips. |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | Come, trouble not yourself, good Master [[characters/fenton\|Fenton]].<br>I will not be your friend nor enemy.<br>My daughter will I question how she loves you,<br>And as I find her, so am I affected.<br>Till then, farewell, sir. She must needs go in.<br>Her father will be angry. |
| [[characters/fenton\|FENTON]] | Farewell, gentle mistress.—Farewell, Nan. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt [[characters/mistress-page\|Mistress Page]] and Anne |
| [[characters/mistress-quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | This is my doing now. ‘Nay’, said I, ‘will you cast away your [[archetypes/child\|child]] on a [[archetypes/fool\|fool]] and a physician? Look on Master [[characters/fenton\|Fenton]].’ This is my doing. |
| [[characters/fenton\|FENTON]] | I thank thee, (giving her a ring) and I pray thee, once<br>tonight<br>Give my sweet Nan this ring. (Giving money) There’s<br>for thy pains. |
| [[characters/mistress-quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | Now heaven send thee good fortune! |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit [[characters/fenton\|Fenton]] |
| [[characters/mistress-quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | A kind heart he hath. A woman would run through [[motifs/fire\|fire]] and [[motifs/water\|water]] for such a kind heart. But yet I would my master had Mistress Anne; or I would Master Slender had her; or, in sooth, I would Master [[characters/fenton\|Fenton]] had her. I will do what I can for them all three, for so I have promised, and I’ll be as good as my word—but speciously for Master [[characters/fenton\|Fenton]]. [[motifs/well\|Well]], I must of another errand to Sir John [[characters/falstaff\|Falstaff]] from my two mistresses. What a beast am I to slack it! Exit |

### Atto 3, Scena 5

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Sir John [[characters/falstaff\|Falstaff]] |
| SIR JOHN | Bardolph, I say! |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Bardolph |
| BARDOLPH | Here, sir. |
| SIR JOHN | Go fetch me a quart of sack; put a toast in’t. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit Bardolph |
| SIR JOHN | Have I lived to be carried in a basket like a barrow of butcher’s offal, and to be thrown in the Thames? [[motifs/well\|Well]], if I be served such another trick, I’ll have my brains ta‘en out and buttered, and give them to a dog for a New Year’s gift. ’Sblood, the rogues slighted me into the [[motifs/river\|river]] with as little remorse as they would have drowned a blind bitch’s puppies, fifteen i’th’ litter! And you may know by my size that I have a kind of alacrity in sinking. If the [[characters/bottom\|bottom]] were as deep as hell, I should down. I had been drowned, but that the shore was shelvy and shallow—a [[motifs/death\|death]] that I abhor, for the [[motifs/water\|water]] swells a man, and what a thing should I have been when I had been swelled? By the Lord, a mountain of mummy! |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Bardolph, with two large cups of  sack |
| BARDOLPH | Here’s [[characters/mistress-quickly\|Mistress Quickly]], sir, to speak with you. |
| SIR JOHN | Come, let me pour in some sack to the Thames’ [[motifs/water\|water]], for my belly’s as cold as if I had swallowed snowballs for pills to cool the reins.<br>He drinks<br>Call her in. |
| BARDOLPH | Come in, woman! |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/mistress-quickly\|Mistress Quickly]] |
| [[characters/mistress-quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | (to Sir John) By your leave; I cry you [[concepts/mercy\|mercy]]. Give your worship good morrow! |
| *(didascalia)* | SIR JOHN (drinking, then speaking to Bardolph) Take away these chalices. Go brew me a pottle of sack, finely. |
| BARDOLPH | With eggs, sir? |
| *(didascalia)* | SIR JOHN Simple of itself. I’ll no pullet-sperms in my brewage. Exit Bardolph, with cups |
| BARDOLPH | How now? |
| [[characters/mistress-quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | Marry, sir, I come to your worship from<br>[[characters/mistress-ford\|Mistress Ford]]. |
| SIR JOHN | [[characters/mistress-ford\|Mistress Ford]]? I have had ford enough: I was thrown into the ford, I have my belly full of ford. |
| [[characters/mistress-quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | Alas the day, good heart, that was not her fault. She does so take on with her men; they mistook their erection. |
| SIR JOHN | So did I mine, to build upon a foolish woman’s promise. |
| [[characters/mistress-quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | [[motifs/well\|Well]], she laments, sir, for it, that it would yearn your heart to see it. Her husband goes this morning a-birding. She desires you once more to come to her, between eight and nine. I must carry her word quickly. She’ll make you amends, I warrant you. |
| SIR JOHN | [[motifs/well\|Well]], I will visit her. Tell her so, and bid her think what a man is; let her consider his frailty, and then judge of my merit. |
| [[characters/mistress-quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | I will tell her. |
| SIR JOHN | Do so. Between nine and ten, sayst thou? |
| [[characters/mistress-quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | Eight and nine, sir. |
| SIR JOHN | [[motifs/well\|Well]], be gone. I will not miss her. |
| [[characters/mistress-quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | Peace be with you, sir. Exit |
| SIR JOHN | I marvel I hear not of Master Brooke; he sent me word to stay within. I like his money [[motifs/well\|well]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/master-ford\|Master Ford]], disguised as Brooke |
| SIR JOHN | By the mass, here he comes. |
| FORD | God bless you, sir. |
| SIR JOHN | Now, Master Brooke, you come to know what hath passed between me and Ford’s wife. |
| FORD | That indeed, Sir John, is my business. |
| SIR JOHN | Master Brooke, I will not lie to you. I was at her house the hour she appointed me. |
| FORD | And sped you, sir? |
| SIR JOHN | Very ill-favouredly, Master Brooke. |
| FORD | How so, sir? Did she change her determination? |
| SIR JOHN | No, Master Brooke, but the peaking cornuto her husband, Master Brooke, dwelling in a continual ’larum of [[concepts/jealousy\|jealousy]], comes me in the instant of our encounter—after we had embraced, kissed, protested, and, as it were, spoke the prologue of our [[forms/comedy\|comedy]]—and at his heels a rabble of his companions, thither provoked and instigated by his distemper, and, forsooth, to search his house for his wife’s [[motifs/love\|love]]. |
| FORD | What, while you were there? |
| SIR JOHN | While I was there. |
| FORD | And did he search for you, and could not find you? |
| SIR JOHN | You shall hear. As God would have it, comes in one [[characters/mistress-page\|Mistress Page]], gives intelligence of Ford’s approach, and, by her invention and Ford’s wife’s distraction, they conveyed me into a buck-basket— |
| FORD | A buck-basket? |
| SIR JOHN | By the Lord, a buck-basket!—rammed me in with foul shirts and smocks, socks, foul stockings, greasy napkins, that, Master Brooke, there was the rankest compound of villainous smell that ever offended nostril. |
| FORD | And how long lay you there? |
| SIR JOHN | Nay, you shall hear, Master Brooke, what I have suffered to bring this woman to evil, for your good. Being thus crammed in the basket, a couple of Ford’s knaves, his hinds, were called forth by their mistress, to carry me, in the name of foul clothes, to Datchet Lane. They took me on their shoulders, met the jealous knave their master in the door, who asked them once or twice what they had in their basket. I quaked for [[concepts/fear\|fear]] lest the lunatic knave would have searched it, but fate, ordaining he should be a cuckold, held his hand. [[motifs/well\|Well]], on went he for a search, and away went I for foul clothes. But mark the sequel, Master Brooke. I suffered the pangs of three several deaths. First, an intolerable fright, to be detected with a jealous rotten bell-wether. Next, to be compassed like a good bilbo in the circumference of a peck, hilt to point, heel to head. And then, to be stopped in, like a strong distillation, with stinking clothes that fretted in their own grease. Think of that—a man of my kidney—think of that—that am as subject to heat as butter, a man of continual dissolution and thaw. It was a miracle to scape suffocation. And in the height of this bath, when I was more than half stewed in grease like a Dutch dish, to be thrown into the Thames and cooled, glowing-hot, in that surge, like a horseshoe. Think of that—hissing hot—think of that, Master Brooke! |
| FORD | In good sadness, sir, I am sorry that for my sake you have suffered all this. My suit then is desperate. You’ll undertake her no more? |
| SIR JOHN | Master Brooke, I will be thrown into Etna as I have been into Thames ere I will leave her thus. Her husband is this morning gone a-birding. I have received from her another embassy of meeting. ’Twixt eight and nine is the hour, Master Brooke. |
| FORD | ’Tis past eight already, sir. |
| SIR JOHN | Is it? I will then address me to my appointment. Come to me at your convenient leisure, and you shall know how I [[characters/speed\|speed]]; and the conclusion shall be crowned with your enjoying her. Adieu. You shall have her, Master Brooke; Master Brooke, you shall cuckold Ford. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit |
| FORD | Hum! Ha! Is this a vision? Is this a dream? Do I [[motifs/sleep\|sleep]]? [[characters/master-ford\|Master Ford]], awake! Awake, [[characters/master-ford\|Master Ford]]! There’s a hole made in your best coat, [[characters/master-ford\|Master Ford]]. This ‘tis to be married! This ’tis to have linen and buck-baskets! [[motifs/well\|Well]], I will proclaim myself what I am. I will now take the lecher. He is at my house. He cannot scape me; ’tis impossible he should. He cannot creep into a halfpenny purse, nor into a pepperbox. But lest the devil that guides him should aid him, I will search impossible places. Though what I am I cannot avoid, yet to be what I would not shall not make me tame. If I have horns to make one mad, let the proverb go with me: I’ll be horn-mad. Exit |

### Atto 4, Scena 1

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/mistress-page\|Mistress Page]], [[characters/mistress-quickly\|Mistress Quickly]], and William Page |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | Is he at [[characters/mistress-ford\|Mistress Ford]]’s already, thinkest thou? |
| [[characters/mistress-quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | Sure he is by this, or will be presently. But truly he is very courageous-mad about his throwing into the [[motifs/water\|water]]. [[characters/mistress-ford\|Mistress Ford]] desires you to come suddenly. |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | I’ll be with her by and by. I’ll but bring my young man here to school. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Sir Hugh Evans |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | Look where his master comes. ’Tis a playing day, I see.—How now, Sir Hugh, no school today? |
| EVANS | No, Master Slender is let the boys leave to play. |
| [[characters/mistress-quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | Blessing of his heart! |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | Sir Hugh, my husband says my son profits nothing in the world at his book. I pray you ask him some questions in his accidence. |
| EVANS | Come hither, William. Hold up your head. Come. |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | Come on, sirrah. Hold up your head.<br>Answer your master; be not afraid. |
| EVANS | William, how many numbers is in nouns? |
| [[characters/mistress-quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | Truly, I thought there had been one number more, because they say ‘’Od’s nouns’. |
| EVANS | Peace your tattlings!—What is ‘fair’, William? |
| WILLIAM | ‘Pulcher’. |
| [[characters/mistress-quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | Polecats? There are fairer things than polecats, sure. |
| EVANS | You are a very simplicity ‘oman. I pray you peace.—What is ‘lapis’, William? |
| WILLIAM | A stone. |
| EVANS | And what is ‘a stone’, William? |
| WILLIAM | A pebble. |
| EVANS | No, it is ‘lapis’. I pray you remember in your prain. |
| WILLIAM | ‘Lapis’. |
| EVANS | That is a good William. What is he, William, that does lend articles? |
| WILLIAM | Articles are borrowed of the pronoun, and be thus declined. Singulariter nominativo: ‘hic, haec, hoc’. |
| EVANS | Nominativo: ‘hig, hag, hog’. Pray you mark: genitivo: ‘huius’. [[motifs/well\|Well]], what is your accusative case? |
| WILLIAM | Accusativo: ‘hinc’— |
| EVANS | I pray you have your remembrance, [[archetypes/child\|child]].<br>Accusativo: ‘hing, hang, hog’. |
| [[characters/mistress-quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | ’Hang-hog’ is Latin for bacon, I warrant you. |
| EVANS | Leave your prabbles, ’oman!—What is the focative case, William? |
| WILLIAM | O—vocativo, O— |
| EVANS | Remember, William, focative is caret. |
| [[characters/mistress-quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | And that’s a good root. |
| EVANS | ’Oman, forbear. |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | (to [[characters/mistress-quickly\|Mistress Quickly]]) Peace. |
| EVANS | What is your genitive case plural, William? |
| WILLIAM | Genitive case? |
| EVANS | Ay. |
| WILLIAM | Genitivo: ‘horum, harum, horum’. |
| [[characters/mistress-quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | Vengeance of Jenny’s case! Fie on her!<br>Never name her, [[archetypes/child\|child]], if she be a whore. |
| EVANS | For shame, ’oman! |
| [[characters/mistress-quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | You do ill to teach the [[archetypes/child\|child]] such words. He teaches him to hick and to hack, which they’ll do fast enough of themselves, and to call ‘whorum’. Fie upon you! |
| EVANS | ’Oman, art thou lunatics? Hast thou no understandings for thy cases, and the numbers of the genders? Thou art as foolish Christian creatures as I would desires. |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | (to [[characters/mistress-quickly\|Mistress Quickly]]) Prithee, hold thy peace. |
| EVANS | Show me now, William, some declensions of your pronouns. |
| WILLIAM | Forsooth, I have forgot. |
| EVANS | It is ‘qui, que, quod’. If you forget your ‘qui’s, your ‘que‘s, and your ‘quod’s, you must be preeches. Go your ways and play; go. |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | He is a better scholar than I thought he was. |
| EVANS | He is a good sprag [[motifs/memory\|memory]]. Farewell, [[characters/mistress-page\|Mistress Page]]. |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | Adieu, good Sir Hugh. Exit Evans<br>Get you [[motifs/home\|home]], boy. Exit William<br><br>(To [[characters/mistress-quickly\|Mistress Quickly]]) Come, we stay too long. Exeunt |

### Atto 4, Scena 2

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Sir John [[characters/falstaff\|Falstaff]] and [[characters/mistress-ford\|Mistress Ford]] |
| SIR JOHN | [[characters/mistress-ford\|Mistress Ford]], your sorrow hath eaten up my sufferance. I see you are obsequious in your [[motifs/love\|love]], and I profess requital to a hair’s breadth: not only, [[characters/mistress-ford\|Mistress Ford]], in the simple office of [[motifs/love\|love]], but in all the accoutrement, complement, and ceremony of it. But are you sure of your husband now? |
| [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] | He’s a-birding, sweet Sir John. |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | (within) What ho, gossip Ford, what ho! |
| [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] | Step into th’ chamber, Sir John.<br>Sir John steps into the chamber |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/mistress-page\|Mistress Page]] |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | How now, sweetheart, who’s at [[motifs/home\|home]] besides yourself? |
| [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] | Why, none but mine own people. |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | Indeed? |
| [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] | No, certainly. (Aside to her) Speak louder. |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | Truly, I am so glad you have nobody here. |
| [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] | Why? |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | Why, woman, your husband is in his old lines again. He so takes on yonder with my husband, so rails against all married mankind, so curses all Eve’s daughters of what complexion soever, and so buffets himself on the forehead, crying ‘Peer out, peer out!’, that any [[concepts/madness\|madness]] I ever yet beheld seemed but tameness, civility, and patience to this his distemper he is in now. I am glad the fat knight is not here. |
| [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] | Why, does he talk of him? |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | Of none but him; and swears he was carried out, the last [[motifs/time\|time]] he searched for him, in a basket, protests to my husband he is now here, and hath drawn him and the rest of their company from their sport to make another experiment of his suspicion. But I am glad the knight is not here. Now he shall see his own foolery. |
| [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] | How near is he, [[characters/mistress-page\|Mistress Page]]? |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | Hard by at street end. He will be here anon. 35 |
| [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] | I am undone: the knight is here. |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | Why then, you are utterly shamed, and he’s but a dead man. What a woman are you! Away with him, away with him! Better shame than murder. |
| [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] | Which way should he go? How should I bestow him? Shall I put him into the basket again?<br>Sir John comes forth from the chamber |
| SIR JOHN | No, I’ll come no more i’th’ basket. May I not go out ere he come? |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | Alas, three of [[characters/master-ford\|Master Ford]]’s brothers watch the door with pistols, that none shall issue out. Otherwise you might slip away ere he came. But what make you here? |
| SIR JOHN | What shall I do? I’ll creep up into the chimney. |
| [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] | There they always use to discharge their birding-pieces. |
| *(didascalia)* | [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] Creep into the kiln-hole. |
| SIR JOHN | Where is it? |
| [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] | He will seek there, on my word. Neither press, coffer, chest, trunk, [[motifs/well\|well]], vault, but he hath an abstract for the remembrance of such places, and goes to them by his note. There is no hiding you in the house. |
| SIR JOHN | I’ll go out, then. |
| *(didascalia)* | [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] If you go out in your own semblance, you die, Sir John—unless you go out disguised. |
| [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] | How might we disguise him? |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | Alas the day, I know not. There is no woman’s gown big enough for him; otherwise he might put on a hat, a muffler, and a kerchief, and so escape. |
| SIR JOHN | Good hearts, devise something. Any extremity rather than a mischief. |
| [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] | My maid’s aunt, the fat woman of Brentford, has a gown above. |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | On my word, it will serve him; she’s as big as he is; and there’s her thrummed hat, and her muffler too.—Run up, Sir John. |
| [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] | Go, go, sweet Sir John. [[characters/mistress-page\|Mistress Page]] and<br>I will look some linen for your head. |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | Quick, quick! We’ll come dress you straight. Put on the gown the while. Exit Sir John |
| [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] | I would my husband would meet him in this shape. He cannot abide the old woman of Brentford. He swears she’s a [[characters/witch\|witch]], forbade her my house, and hath threatened to beat her. |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | Heaven guide him to thy husband’s cudgel, and the devil guide his cudgel afterwards! |
| [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] | But is my husband coming? |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | Ay, in good sadness is he, and talks of the basket too, howsoever he hath had intelligence. |
| [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] | We’ll try that, for I’ll appoint my men to carry the basket again, to meet him at the door with it as they did last [[motifs/time\|time]]. |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | Nay, but he’ll be here presently. Let’s go dress him like the [[characters/witch\|witch]] of Brentford. |
| [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] | I’ll first direct my men what they shall do with the basket. Go up; I’ll bring linen for him straight. |
| *(didascalia)* | [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] Hang him, dishonest varlet ! We cannot misuse him enough. Exit [[characters/mistress-ford\|Mistress Ford]] |
| [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] | We’ll leave a proof by that which we will do,<br>Wives may be merry, and yet honest, too.<br>We do not act that often jest and laugh.<br>’Tis old but true: ’Still swine eats all the draff’. Exit |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/mistress-ford\|Mistress Ford]], with John and Robert |
| [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] | Go, sirs, take the basket again on your shoulders. Your master is hard at door. If he bid you set it down, obey him. Quickly, dispatch! Exit |
| *(didascalia)* | JOHN Come, come, take it up. |
| *(didascalia)* | ROBERT Pray heaven it be not full of knight again. |
| *(didascalia)* | JOHN I [[concepts/hope\|hope]] not; I had as lief bear so much lead. |
| [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] | They lift the basket. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/master-ford\|Master Ford]], [[characters/master-page\|Master Page]], [[characters/doctor-caius\|Doctor Caius]], Sir |
| [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] | Hugh Evans, and [[characters/justice-shallow\|Justice Shallow]] |
| FORD | Ay, but if it prove true, [[characters/master-page\|Master Page]], have you any way then to unfool me again? (To John and Robert) Set down the basket, villains.<br>John and Robert set down the basket<br>Somebody call my wife. Youth in a basket! ! O, you panderly rascals! There’s a knot, a gang, a pack, a conspiracy against me. Now shall the devil be shamed.—What, wife, I say! Come, come forth! Behold what honest clothes you send forth to bleaching. |
| PAGE | Why, this passes, [[characters/master-ford\|Master Ford]]. You are not to go loose any longer; you must be pinioned. |
| EVANS | Why, this is lunatics; this is mad as a mad dog. |
| SHALLOW | Indeed, [[characters/master-ford\|Master Ford]], this is not [[motifs/well\|well]], indeed. |
| FORD | So say I too, sir. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/mistress-ford\|Mistress Ford]] |
| FORD | Come hither, [[characters/mistress-ford\|Mistress Ford]] ! [[characters/mistress-ford\|Mistress Ford]], the honest woman, the modest wife, the virtuous creature, that hath the jealous [[archetypes/fool\|fool]] to her husband I suspect without cause, mistress, do I? |
| [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] | God be my witness you do, if you suspect me in any dishonesty. |
| FORD | [[motifs/well\|Well]] said, brazen-face; hold it out.<br>He opens the basket and starts to take out clothes<br>Come forth, sirrah! |
| PAGE | This passes. |
| [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] | (to Ford) Are you not ashamed? Let the clothes alone. |
| FORD | I shall find you anon. |
| EVANS | ’Tis unreasonable: will you take up your wife’s clothes? Come, away. |
| *(didascalia)* | FORD to John and Robert Empty the basket, I say. |
| *(didascalia)* | PAGE Why, man, why? |
| *(didascalia)* | FORD [[characters/master-page\|Master Page]], as I am a man, there was one conveyed out of my house yesterday in this basket. Why may not he be there again? In my house I am sure he is. My intelligence is true, my [[concepts/jealousy\|jealousy]] is reasonable. To John and Robert Pluck me out all the linen. |
| EVANS | He takes out clothes |
| [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] | If you find a man there, he shall die a flea’s [[motifs/death\|death]]. |
| PAGE | Here’s no man. |
| SHALLOW | By my fidelity, this is not [[motifs/well\|well]], [[characters/master-ford\|Master Ford]].<br>This wrongs you. |
| EVANS | [[characters/master-ford\|Master Ford]], you must pray, and not follow the imaginations of your own heart. This is jealousies. |
| FORD | [[motifs/well\|Well]], he’s not here I seek for. |
| PAGE | No, nor nowhere else but in your brain. |
| *(didascalia)* | FORD Help to search my house this one [[motifs/time\|time]]. If I find not what I seek, show no colour for my extremity; let me for ever be your table-sport; let them say of me, ‘As jealous as Ford, that searched a hollow walnut for his wife’s leman’. Satisfy me once more; once more search with me. Exeunt John and Robert with the basket |
| [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] | What ho, [[characters/mistress-page\|Mistress Page]]! Come you and the old woman down. My husband will come into the chamber. |
| FORD | Old woman? What old woman’s that? |
| [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] | Why, it is my maid’s Aunt of Brentford. |
| FORD | A [[characters/witch\|witch]], a quean, an old, cozening quean! ! Have I not forbid her my house? She comes of errands, does she? We are simple men; we do not know what’s brought to pass under the profession of fortune-telling. She works by charms, by spells, by th’ figure, and such daubery as this is, beyond our element. We know nothing.—Come down, you [[characters/witch\|witch]], you hag, you ! Come down, I say! |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/mistress-page\|Mistress Page]], and Sir John [[characters/falstaff\|Falstaff]], |
| *(didascalia)* | disguised as an old woman. |
| *(didascalia)* | Ford makes towards them |
| [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] | Nay, good sweet husband!—Good gentlemen, let him not strike the old woman. |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | (to Sir John) Come, Mother Prat. Come, give me your hand. |
| FORD | I’ll prat her!<br>He beats Sir John<br>Out of my door, you [[characters/witch\|witch]], you rag, you baggage, you polecat, you runnion! Out, out! I’ll conjure you, I’ll fortune-tell you! Exit Sir John |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | Are you not ashamed? I think you have killed the poor woman. |
| [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] | Nay, he will do it.—’Tis a goodly credit for you! |
| FORD | Hang her, [[characters/witch\|witch]]! |
| EVANS | By Jeshu, I think the ‘oman is a [[characters/witch\|witch]] indeed. I like not when a ’oman has a great peard. I spy a great peard under his muffler. |
| FORD | Will you follow, gentlemen? I beseech you, follow. See but the issue of my [[concepts/jealousy\|jealousy]]. If I cry out thus upon no trail, never trust me when I open again. |
| PAGE | Let’s obey his humour a little further. Come, gentlemen. Exeunt the men |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | By my troth, he beat him most pitifully. |
| [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] | Nay, by th’ mass, that he did not—he beat him most unpitifully, methought. |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | I’ll have the cudgel hallowed and hung o’er the altar. It hath done meritorious service. |
| [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] | What think you—may we, with the warrant of womanhood and the witness of a good [[concepts/conscience\|conscience]], pursue him with any further [[concepts/revenge\|revenge]]? |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | The spirit of wantonness is sure scared out of him. If the devil have him not in fee-simple, with fine and recovery, he will never, I think, in the way of waste attempt us again. |
| [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] | Shall we tell our husbands how we have served him? |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | Yes, by all means, if it be but to scrape the figures out of your husband’s brains. If they can find in their hearts the poor, unvirtuous, fat knight shall be any further afflicted, we two will still be the ministers. |
| [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] | I’ll warrant they’ll have him publicly shamed, and methinks there would be no period to the jest should he not be publicly shamed. |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | Come, to the forge with it, then shape it. I would not have things cool. Exeunt |

### Atto 4, Scena 3

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the Host of the Garter and Bardolph |
| BARDOLPH | Sir, the Germans [[motifs/desire\|desire]] to have three of your horses. The Duke himself will be tomorrow at [[settings/court\|court]], and they are going to meet him. |
| HOST | What duke should that be comes so secretly? I hear not of him in the [[settings/court\|court]]. Let me speak with the gentlemen. They speak English? |
| BARDOLPH | Ay, sir. I’ll call them to you. |
| HOST | They shall have my horses, but I’ll make them pay; I’ll sauce them. They have had my house a week at command; I have turned away my other guests. They must come off: I’ll sauce them. Come. Exeunt |

### Atto 4, Scena 4

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/master-page\|Master Page]], [[characters/master-ford\|Master Ford]], [[characters/mistress-page\|Mistress Page]], [[characters/mistress-ford\|Mistress Ford]], and Sir Hugh Evans |
| EVANS | ‘Tis one of the best discretions of a ’oman as ever I did look upon. |
| PAGE | And did he send you both these letters at an instant? |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | Within a quarter of an hour. |
| FORD | Pardon me, wife. Henceforth do what thou wilt.<br>I rather will suspect the sun with cold<br>Than thee with wantonness. Now doth thy [[concepts/honour\|honour]]<br>stand,<br>In him that was of late an heretic,<br>As firm as [[concepts/faith\|faith]]. |
| PAGE | ’Tis [[motifs/well\|well]], ’tis [[motifs/well\|well]]; no more.<br>Be not as extreme in submission<br>As in offence.<br>But let our plot go forward. Let our wives<br>Yet once again, to make us public sport,<br>Appoint a meeting with this old fat fellow,<br>Where we may take him and disgrace him for it. |
| FORD | There is no better way than that they spoke of. |
| PAGE | How, to send him word they’ll meet him in the Park<br>At midnight? Fie, fie, he’ll never come. |
| EVANS | You say he has been thrown in the rivers, and has been grievously peaten as an old ’oman. Methinks there should be terrors in him, that he should not come. Methinks his flesh is punished; he shall have no desires. |
| PAGE | So think I too. |
| *(didascalia)* | [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] |
| PAGE | Devise but how you’ll use him when he comes,<br>And let us two devise to bring him thither. |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | There is an old tale goes that Herne the hunter,<br>Sometime a keeper here in Windsor Forest,<br>Doth all the [[motifs/winter\|winter]] [[motifs/time\|time]] at still midnight<br>Walk round about an oak with great ragg’d horns;<br>And there he blasts the trees, and takes the cattle,<br>And makes milch-kine yield [[motifs/blood\|blood]], and shakes a chain<br>In a most hideous and dreadful manner.<br>You have heard of such a spirit, and [[motifs/well\|well]] you know<br>The superstitious idle-headed eld<br>Received, and did deliver to our age,<br>This tale of Herne the hunter for a [[concepts/truth\|truth]]. |
| PAGE | Why, yet there want not many that do [[concepts/fear\|fear]]<br>In deep of [[motifs/night\|night]] to walk by this Herne’s Oak.<br>But what of this? |
| [[characters/mistress-ford\|MISTRESS FORD]] | Marry, this is our device:<br>That [[characters/falstaff\|Falstaff]] at that oak shall meet with us,<br>Disguised like Herne, with huge horns on his head. |
| PAGE | [[motifs/well\|Well]], let it not be doubted but he’ll come,<br>And in this shape. When you have brought him<br>thither<br>What shall be done with him? What is your plot? |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | That likewise have we thought upon, and thus.<br>Nan Page my daughter, and my little son,<br>And three or four more of their growth, we’ll dress<br>Like urchins, oafs, and fairies, green and white,<br>With rounds of waxen tapers on their heads,<br>And rattles in their hands. Upon a sudden,<br>As [[characters/falstaff\|Falstaff]], she, and I are newly met,<br>Let them from forth a saw-pit rush at once,<br>With some diffused song. Upon their sight<br>We two in great amazèdness will fly.<br>Then let them all encircle him about,<br>And, fairy-like, to pinch the unclean knight,<br>And ask him why, that hour of fairy revel,<br>In their so sacred paths he dares to tread<br>In shape profane.<br>[mistress! FORD And till he tell the [[concepts/truth\|truth]],<br>Let the supposed fairies pinch him sound,<br>And burn him with their tapers. |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | The [[concepts/truth\|truth]] being known,<br>We’ll all present ourselves, dis-horn the spirit,<br>And mock him [[motifs/home\|home]] to Windsor. |
| FORD | The children must<br>Be practised [[motifs/well\|well]] to this, or they’ll ne’er do’t. |
| EVANS | I will teach the children their behaviours, and I will be like a jackanapes also, to burn the knight with my taber. |
| FORD | That will be excellent. I’ll go buy them vizors. |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | My Nan shall be the [[characters/queen\|Queen]] of all the Fairies,<br>Finely attired in a robe of white. |
| PAGE | That silk will I go buy—(aside) and in that tire<br>Shall Master Slender steal my Nan away,<br>And marry her at Eton. (To [[characters/mistress-page\|Mistress Page]]) Go send to<br>[[characters/falstaff\|Falstaff]] straight. |
| FORD | Nay, I’ll to him again in name of Brooke.<br>He’ll tell me all his purpose. Sure he’ll come. |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | [[concepts/fear\|Fear]] not you that. (To Page, Ford, and Evans) Go get us<br>properties<br>And tricking for our fairies. |
| EVANS | Let us about it. It is admirable pleasures, and fery honest knaveries. Exeunt Ford, Page, and Evans |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | Go, [[characters/mistress-ford\|Mistress Ford]],<br>Send quickly to Sir John, to know his mind. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit [[characters/mistress-ford\|Mistress Ford]] |
| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | I’ll to the Doctor. He hath my good will,<br>And none but he, to marry with Nan Page.<br>That Slender, though [[motifs/well\|well]] landed, is an idiot;<br>And he my husband best of all affects.<br>The Doctor is [[motifs/well\|well]] moneyed, and his friends<br>Potent at [[settings/court\|court]]. He, none but he, shall have her,<br>Though twenty thousand worthier come to crave her. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit |

### Atto 4, Scena 5

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter the Host of the Garter and Simple |
| HOST | What wouldst thou have, boor? What, thick-skin? Speak, breathe, discuss. Brief, short, quick, snap. |
| SIMPLE | Marry, sir, I come to speak with Sir John [[characters/falstaff\|Falstaff]], from Master Slender. |
| HOST | There’s his chamber, his house, his [[settings/castle\|castle]], his standing-bed and truckle-bed. ’Tis painted about with the story of the Prodigal, fresh and new. Go knock and call. He’ll speak like an Anthropophaginian unto thee. Knock, I say. |
| SIMPLE | There’s an old woman, a fat woman, gone up into his chamber. I’ll be so bold as stay, sir, till she come down. I come to speak with her, indeed. |
| HOST | Ha, a fat woman? The knight may be robbed. I’ll call.—Bully knight, bully Sir John! Speak from thy lungs military! Art thou there? It is thine Host, thine Ephesian, calls. |
| SIR JOHN | (within) How now, mine Host? |
| HOST | Here’s a Bohemian Tartar tarries the coming down of thy fat woman. Let her descend, bully, let her descend. My chambers are honourable. Fie, privacy! Fie! |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Sir John [[characters/falstaff\|Falstaff]] |
| SIR JOHN | There was, mine Host, an old fat woman even now with me; but she’s gone. |
| SIMPLE | Pray you, sir, was’t not the wise woman of Brentford? |
| SIR JOHN | Ay, marry was it, mussel-shell. What would you with her? |
| SIMPLE | My master, sir, my master Slender, sent to her, seeing her go through the streets, to know, sir, whether one Nim, sir, that beguiled him of a chain, had the chain or no. |
| SIR JOHN | I spake with the old woman about it. |
| SIMPLE | And what says she, I pray, sir? |
| SIR JOHN | Marry, she says that the very same man that beguiled Master Slender of his chain cozened him of it. |
| SIMPLE | I would I could have spoken with the woman herself. I had other things to have spoken with her, too, from him. |
| SIR JOHN | What are they? Let us know. |
| HOST | Ay, come, quick. |
| *(didascalia)* | SIMPLE I may not conceal them, sir. |
| HOST | Conceal them, or thou diest. |
| SIMPLE | Why, sir, they were nothing but about Mistress [[characters/anne-page\|Anne Page]], to know if it were my master’s fortune to have her or no. |
| SIR JOHN | ’Tis, ’tis his fortune. |
| SIMPLE | What, sir? |
| SIR JOHN | To have her or no. Go say the woman told me SO. |
| SIMPLE | May I be bold to say so, sir? |
| SIR JOHN | Ay, Sir Tike; who more bold? |
| SIMPLE | I thank your worship. I shall make my master glad with these tidings. Exit |
| HOST | Thou art clerkly, thou art clerkly, Sir John. Was there a wise woman with thee? |
| SIR JOHN | Ay, that there was, mine Host, one that hath taught me more wit than ever I learned before in my life. And I paid nothing for it, neither, but was paid for my learning. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Bardolph, muddy |
| BARDOLPH | O Lord, sir, cozenage, mere cozenagel |
| HOST | Where be my horses? Speak [[motifs/well\|well]] of them, varletto. |
| BARDOLPH | Run away with the cozeners. For so soon as I came beyond Eton, they threw me off from behind one of them, in a slough of mire, and set spurs and away, like three German devils, three Doctor Faustuses. |
| HOST | They are gone but to meet the Duke, [[archetypes/villain\|villain]]. Do not say they be fled. Germans are honest men. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Sir Hugh Evans |
| EVANS | Where is mine Host? |
| HOST | What is the matter, sir? |
| EVANS | Have a care of your entertainments. There is a friend of mine come to town tells me there is three cozen Garmombles that has cozened all the hosts of Reading, of Maidenhead, of Colnbrook, of horses and money. I tell you for good will, look you. You are wise, and full of gibes and vlouting-stocks, and ’tis not convenient you should be cozened. Fare you [[motifs/well\|well]]. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exit |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/doctor-caius\|Doctor Caius]] |
| CAIUS | Vere is mine Host de Jarteer? |
| HOST | Here, Master Doctor, in perplexity and doubtful dilemma. |
| CAIUS | I cannot tell vat is dat, but it is tell-a me dat you make grand preparation for a duke de Jamany. By my trot, der is no duke that the [[settings/court\|court]] is know to come. I tell you for good will. Adieu. Exit |
| HOST | (to Bardolph) Hue and cry, [[archetypes/villain\|villain]], go! (To Sir John) Assist me, knight. I am undone. (To Bardolph) Fly, run, hue and cry, [[archetypes/villain\|villain]]. I am undone. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt Host and Bardolph severally |
| SIR JOHN | I would all the world might be cozened, for I have been cozened, and beaten too. If it should come to the ear of the [[settings/court\|court]] how I have been transformed, and how my transformation hath been washed and cudgelled, they would melt me out of my fat, drop by drop, and liquor fishermen’s boots with me. I warrant they would whip me with their fine wits till I were as crestfallen as a dried pear. I never prospered since I forswore myself at primero. [[motifs/well\|Well]], if my [[motifs/wind\|wind]] were but long enough, I would repent. |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/mistress-quickly\|Mistress Quickly]] |
| SIR JOHN | Now; whence come you? |
| [[characters/mistress-quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | From the two parties, forsooth. |
| SIR JOHN | The devil take one party, and his dam the other, and so they shall be both bestowed. I have suffered more for their sakes, more than the villainous inconstancy of man’s disposition is able to bear. |
| [[characters/mistress-quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | O Lord, sir, and have not they suffered? Yes, I warrant, speciously one of them. [[characters/mistress-ford\|Mistress Ford]], good heart, is beaten black and blue, that you cannot see a white spot about her. |
| SIR JOHN | What tellest thou me of black and blue? I was beaten myself into all the colours of the rainbow, and I was like to be apprehended for the [[characters/witch\|witch]] of Brentford. But that my admirable dexterity of wit, my counterfeiting the action of an old woman, delivered me, the knave constable had set me i‘th’ stocks, i’th’ common stocks, for a [[characters/witch\|witch]]. |
| [[characters/mistress-quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | Sir, let me speak with you in your chamber. You shall hear how things go, and, I warrant, to your content. Here is a letter will say somewhat. Good hearts, what ado here is to bring you together! Sure one of you does not serve heaven [[motifs/well\|well]], that you are so crossed. |
| SIR JOHN | Come up into my chamber. Exeunt 4.6 |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Master [[characters/fenton\|Fenton]] and the Host of the Garter HOST Master [[characters/fenton\|Fenton]], talk not to me. My mind is heavy. |
| SIR JOHN | I will give over all. |
| [[characters/fenton\|FENTON]] | Yet hear me speak. Assist me in my purpose,<br>And, as I am a gentleman, I’ll give thee<br>A hundred pound in gold more than your loss. |
| HOST | I will hear you, Master [[characters/fenton\|Fenton]], and I will at the least keep your counsel. |
| [[characters/fenton\|FENTON]] | From [[motifs/time\|time]] to [[motifs/time\|time]] I have acquainted you<br>With the dear [[motifs/love\|love]] I bear to fair [[characters/anne-page\|Anne Page]],<br>Who mutually hath answered my affection,<br>So far forth as herself might be her chooser,<br>Even to my wish. I have a letter from her<br>Of such contents as you will [[concepts/wonder\|wonder]] at,<br>The mirth whereof so larded with my matter<br>That neither singly can be manifested<br>Without the show of both. Fat [[characters/falstaff\|Falstaff]]<br>Hath a great scene. The image of the jest<br>I’ll show you here at large. Hark, good mine Host.<br>Tonight at Herne’s Oak, just ‘twixt twelve and one,<br>Must my sweet Nan present the Fairy [[characters/queen\|Queen]]— |
| *(didascalia)* | Showing the letter |
| [[characters/fenton\|FENTON]] | The purpose why is here—in which disguise,<br>While other jests are something rank on foot,<br>Her father hath commanded her to slip<br>Away with Slender, and with him at Eton<br>Immediately to marry. She hath consented.<br>Now, sir, her mother, ever strong against that match<br>And firm for [[characters/doctor-caius\|Doctor Caius]], hath appointed<br>That he shall likewise shuffle her away,<br>While other sports are tasking of their minds,<br>And at the dean’ry, where a priest attends,<br>Straight marry her. To this her mother’s plot<br>She, seemingly obedient, likewise hath<br>Made promise to the Doctor. Now, thus it rests.<br>Her father means she shall be all in white;<br>And in that habit, when Slender sees his [[motifs/time\|time]]<br>To take her by the hand and bid her go,<br>She shall go with him. Her mother hath intended,<br>The better to denote her to the Doctor—<br>For they must all be masked and visored—<br>That quaint in green she shall be loose enrobed,<br>With ribbons pendant flaring ’bout her head;<br>And when the Doctor spies his vantage ripe,<br>To pinch her by the hand, and on that token<br>The maid hath given consent to go with him. |
| HOST | Which means she to deceive, father or mother? |
| [[characters/fenton\|FENTON]] | Both, my good Host, to go along with me.<br>And here it rests: that you’ll procure the vicar<br>To stay for me at church ’twixt twelve and one,<br>And, in the lawful name of marrying,<br>To give our hearts united ceremony. |
| HOST | [[motifs/well\|Well]], husband your device. I’ll to the vicar.<br>Bring you the maid, you shall not lack a priest. |
| [[characters/fenton\|FENTON]] | So shall I evermore be bound to thee.<br>Besides, I’ll make a present recompense. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt severally |

### Atto 5, Scena 1

| Speaker | Line |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Enter Sir John [[characters/falstaff\|Falstaff]] and [[characters/mistress-quickly\|Mistress Quickly]] |
| SIR JOHN | Prithee, no more prattling; go; I’ll hold. This is the third [[motifs/time\|time]]; I [[concepts/hope\|hope]] good luck lies in odd numbers. Away, go! They say there is divinity in odd numbers, either in nativity, chance, or [[motifs/death\|death]]. Away! |
| [[characters/mistress-quickly\|MISTRESS QUICKLY]] | I’ll provide you a chain, and I’ll do what I can to get you a pair of horns. |
| SIR JOHN | Away, I say! [[motifs/time\|Time]] wears. Hold up your head, and mince. Exit [[characters/mistress-quickly\|Mistress Quickly]] |
| *(didascalia)* | Enter [[characters/master-ford\|Master Ford]], disguised as Brooke |
| SIR JOHN | How now, Master Brooke ? Master Brooke, the matter will be known tonight or never. Be you in the Park about midnight at Herne’s Oak, and you shall see wonders. |
| FORD | Went you not to her yesterday, sir, as you told me you had appointed? |
| SIR JOHN | I went to her, Master Brooke, as you see, like a poor old man; but I came from her, Master Brooke, like a poor old woman. That same knave Ford, her husband, hath the finest mad devil of [[concepts/jealousy\|jealousy]] in him, Master Brooke, that ever governed frenzy. I will tell you, he beat me grievously in the shape of a woman—for in the shape of man, Master Brooke, I [[concepts/fear\|fear]] not Goliath with a weaver’s beam, because I know also life is a shuttle. I am in haste. Go along with me; I’ll tell you all, Master Brooke. Since I plucked geese, played truant, and whipped top, I knew not what ’twas to be beaten till lately. Follow me. I’ll tell you strange things of this knave Ford, on whom tonight I will be revenged, and I will deliver his wife into your hand. Follow. Strange things in hand, Master Brooke. Follow. |
| *(didascalia)* | Exeunt |

| SLENDER | Ay, forsooth. I have spoke with her, and we have a nay-word how to know one another. I come to her in white and cry ‘mum’; she cries ‘budget’; and by that we know one another. |

O omnipotent [[motifs/love\|love]]! How near the god drew to the complexion of a goose ! A fault done first in the form of a beast—O Jove, a beastly fault!—and then another fault in the semblance of a fowl—think on‘t, Jove, a foul fault! When gods have hot backs, what shall poor men do? | *(didascalia)* | [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] |
| PAGE | Why went you not with Master Doctor, maid? |
| [[characters/fenton\|FENTON]] | You do amaze her. Hear the [[concepts/truth\|truth]] of it.<br>You would have married her, most shamefully,<br>Where there was no proportion held in [[motifs/love\|love]].<br>The [[concepts/truth\|truth]] is, she and I, long since contracted,<br>Are now so sure that nothing can dissolve us.<br>Th’offence is holy that she hath committed,<br>And this deceit loses the name of craft,<br>Of disobedience, or unduteous title,<br>Since therein she doth evitate and shun<br>A thousand irreligious cursed hours<br>Which forced [[motifs/marriage\|marriage]] would have brought upon her. |

| SIR JOHN | I am glad, though you have ta’en a special stand to strike at me, that your arrow hath glanced. |
| PAGE | [[motifs/well\|Well]], what remedy? [[characters/fenton\|Fenton]], heaven give thee joy I<br>What cannot be eschewed must be embraced. |

| [[characters/mistress-page\|MISTRESS PAGE]] | [[motifs/well\|Well]], I will muse no further. Master [[characters/fenton\|Fenton]],<br>Heaven give you many, many merry days!<br>Good husband, let us every one go [[motifs/home\|home]],<br>And laugh this sport o’er by a country [[motifs/fire\|fire]],<br>Sir John and all. |
| FORD | Let it be so, Sir John.<br>To Master Brooke you yet shall hold your word,<br>For he tonight shall lie with [[characters/mistress-ford\|Mistress Ford]]. Exeunt<br><br>2 [[characters/henry-iv\|HENRY IV]]<br>2<br>[[characters/henry-iv\|Henry IV]], printed in 1600 as The Second Part of Henry the Fourth, was not reprinted until it was included in somewhat revised form in the 1623 Folio, with the same title. Shakespeare may have started to write it in 1597, directly after I [[characters/henry-iv\|Henry IV]], but have laid it aside while he composed The Merry Wives of Windsor. As in I [[characters/henry-iv\|Henry IV]], he drew on The Famous Victories of Henry the Fifth, Holinshed’s Chronicles, and Samuel Daniel’s Four Books of the Civil Wars, along with other, minor sources; but the play contains a greater proportion of non-historical material apparently invented by Shakespeare. In this play Shakespeare seems from the start to have accepted the change of Sir John’s surname to [[characters/falstaff\|Falstaff]] which had been enforced upon him in I [[characters/henry-iv\|Henry IV]].<br>Like I [[characters/henry-iv\|Henry IV]], Part Two draws on the techniques of [[forms/comedy\|comedy]], but its overall tone is more sombre. At its start, the Prince seems to have regressed from his reformed state at the end of Part One; his father still has many causes for anxiety, has not made his expiatory pilgrimage to the Holy Land, and is again the victim of rebellion, led this [[motifs/time\|time]] by the [[characters/earl-of-northumberland\|Earl of Northumberland]], the Archbishop of York, and the Lords [[characters/hastings\|Hastings]] and [[characters/mowbray\|Mowbray]]. Again Henry’s public responsibilities are exacerbated by anxieties about Prince Harry’s behaviour; the climax of their relationship comes after Harry, discovering his sick father asleep and thinking him dead, tries on his [[motifs/crown\|crown]]; after bitterly upbraiding him, Henry accepts his son’s assertions of good [[concepts/faith\|faith]], and, recalling the devious means by which he himself came to the throne, warns Harry that he may need to protect himself against civil strife by pursuing ‘foreign quarrels’-the campaigning against France depicted in [[characters/henry-v\|Henry V]]. The [[archetypes/king\|King]] dies in the Jerusalem Chamber of Westminster Abbey, the closest he will get to the Holy Land.<br>In this play the Prince spends less [[motifs/time\|time]] than in Part One with Sir John, who is shown much in the company of [[characters/mistress-quickly\|Mistress Quickly]] and [[characters/doll-tearsheet\|Doll Tearsheet]] at the Boar’s Head tavern in Eastcheap and later in Gloucestershire on his way to and from the place of battle. Shakespeare never excelled the bitter-sweet [[forms/comedy\|comedy]] of the passages involving [[characters/falstaff\|Falstaff]] and his old comrade [[characters/justice-shallow\|Justice Shallow]]. The play ends in a counterpointing of major and minor keys as the newly crowned [[characters/henry-v\|Henry V]] rejects Sir John and all that he has stood for. |


<span class="qlang-split" data-lang="it"></span>

THE MERRY WIVES OF WINDSOR
A LEGEND dating from 1702 claims that Shakespeare wrote The Merry Wives of Windsor in fourteen days and by command of [[characters/queen-elizabeth|Queen Elizabeth]]; in 1709 she was said to have wished particularly to see [[characters/falstaff|Falstaff]] in [[motifs/love|love]]. Whether or not this is true, a passage towards the end of the play alluding directly to the ceremonies of the Order of the Garter, Britain’s highest order of chivalry, encourages the belief that the play has a direct connection with a specific occasion. In 1597 George Carey, Lord Hunsdon, Lord Chamberlain and patron of Shakespeare’s company, was installed at Windsor as a Knight of the Garter. The [[characters/queen|Queen]] was not present at the installation but had attended the Garter Feast at the Palace of Westminster on St George’s Day (23 April). Shakespeare’s play was probably performed in association with this occasion, and may have been written especially for it. It was first printed, in a corrupt text, in 1602; a better text appears in the 1623 Folio.
Some of the characters—Sir John [[characters/falstaff|Falstaff]], [[characters/mistress-quickly|Mistress Quickly]], [[characters/pistol|Pistol]], Nim, [[characters/justice-shallow|Justice Shallow]]—appear also in I and 2 [[characters/henry-iv|Henry IV]] and [[characters/henry-v|Henry V]], but in spite of a reference to ’the wild Prince and Poins’ at 3.2.66-7, this is essentially an Elizabethan [[forms/comedy|comedy]], the only one that Shakespeare set firmly in England. The play is full of details that would have been familiar to Elizabethan Londoners, and the language is colloquial and up to date. The plot, however, is made up of conventional situations whose ancestry is literary rather than realistic. There are many analogues to Shakespeare’s basic plot situations in medieval and other tales, some in books that he probably or certainly knew. The central story, of Sir John’s unsuccessful attempts to seduce [[characters/mistress-page|Mistress Page]] and [[characters/mistress-ford|Mistress Ford]], and of [[characters/master-ford|Master Ford]]’s unfounded [[concepts/jealousy|jealousy]], is in the tradition of the Italian novella, and may have been suggested by Ser Giovanni Fiorentino’s II Pecorone (1558). Alongside it Shakespeare places the comical but finally [[concepts/romantic-love|romantic love]] story of [[characters/anne-page|Anne Page]], wooed by the foolish but rich Abraham Slender and the irascible French [[characters/doctor-caius|Doctor Caius]], but won by the young and handsome [[characters/fenton|Fenton]]. The play contains a higher proportion of prose to verse than any other play by Shakespeare, and the action is often broadly comic; but it ends, after the midnight scene in Windsor Forest during which Sir John is frightened out of his lechery, in [[concepts/forgiveness|forgiveness]] and [[motifs/love|love]].
The Merry Wives of Windsor is known to have been acted for James I on 4 November 1604, and for Charles I in 1638. It was revived soon after the theatres reopened, in 1660; at first it was not particularly popular, but since 1720 it has consistently pleased audiences. Many artists have illustrated it, and it forms the basis for a number of operas, including Otto Nicolai’s Die lustigen Weiber von Windsor (1849) and Giuseppe Verdi’s comic masterpiece, [[characters/falstaff|Falstaff]] (1893).

THE PERSONS OF THE PLAY

The Merry Wives of Windsor

### Atto 1, Scena 1

| Chi parla | Battuta |
|---|---|
| *(didascalia)* | Entrano [[characters/justice-shallow\|il giudice Shallow]], mastro Slender e Sir Hugh Evans |
| SHALLOW | Sir Hugh, non cercate di dissuadermi. Ne farò una causa da Camera Stellata. Fossero anche venti Sir John Falstaff, non farà oltraggio a Robert Shallow, scudiero. |
| SLENDER | Nella contea di [[characters/gloucester\|Gloucester]], giudice di pace e Coram. |
| SHALLOW | Sì, cugino Slender, e Custalorum. |
| SLENDER | Sì, e Ratolorum anche; e gentiluomo di nascita, mastro parroco, che si firma ‘Armigero’ in ogni atto, mandato, quietanza o obbligazione: ‘Armigero’. |
| SHALLOW | Sì, così faccio, e così ho fatto in ogni [[motifs/time\|tempo]] da trecento anni a questa parte. |
| SLENDER | Tutti i suoi successori andati prima di lui l’hanno fatto, e tutti i suoi antenati che verranno dopo di lui lo potranno fare. Possono portare le dodici lucce bianche nel loro stemma. |
| SHALLOW | È un vecchio stemma. |
| EVANS | Le todici tozzine pianche stanno [[motifs/well\|pene]] sopra un fecchio stemma. S’accortano [[motifs/well\|pene]] passante: è pestia familiare all’uomo, e significa [[motifs/love\|amore]]. |
| SHALLOW | La luccia è il pesce fresco; il pesce salato è un vecchio merluzzo. |
| SLENDER | Posso inquartare, cugino. |
| SHALLOW | Potete, sposandovi. |
| EVANS | È uno sguartare, in verità, se lo inquarta. |
| SHALLOW | Neanche un briciolo. |
| EVANS | Sì, per la Matonna. Se ha un quarto del vostro stemma, non restano che tre falde per voi, secondo le mie semplici congetture. Ma è tutt’uno. Se Sir John [[characters/falstaff\|Falstaff]] ha commesso tisprezzamenti contro di voi, io sono della Chiesa, e sarò lieto di prestare la mia penevolenza per fare espiazioni e compromessi tra voi. |
| SHALLOW | Il Consiglio ne sarà informato; è una sommossa. |
| EVANS | Non è confeniente che il Consiglio senta una sommossa. Non c’è [[concepts/fear\|timore]] di Tio in una sommossa. Il Consiglio, fedete, [[motifs/desire\|tesitererà]] udire il [[concepts/fear\|timore]] di Tio, e non udire una sommossa. Prendete i vostri ’fvisamenti su questo. |
| SHALLOW | Ah! Sulla mia vita, se fossi giovane ancora, la spada la finirebbe. |
| EVANS | È meglio che gli amici siano la spata e la finiscano. E c’è anche un altro tisegno nel mio cerfello, che forse porta con sé pone tiscrezioni. C’è [[characters/anne-page\|Anne Page]] che è figlia di mastro George Page, che è graziosa ferginità. |
| SLENDER | Madonna [[characters/anne-page\|Anne Page]]? Ha i capelli castani, e parla sottile come una donna? |
| EVANS | È quella fera persona in tutto il monto, giusto come potrete [[motifs/desire\|tesiterare]]. E settecento sterline di tanaro, e oro e argento, glieli tà suo nonno sul suo letto di [[motifs/death\|morte]]—Tio lo conduca a una gioiosa risurrezioni—quando ella sarà in grado di raggiungere i diciassette anni. Sarepe una pona mozione se lasciassimo i nostri pisticci e pasticci, e [[motifs/desire\|tesiterassimo]] un [[motifs/marriage\|matrimonio]] tra mastro Abraham e madonna [[characters/anne-page\|Anne Page]]. |
| SLENDER | Il nonno le ha lasciato settecento sterline? |
| *(didascalia)* | EVANS Sì, e suo padre le farà un gruzzolo ancora pon migliore. SHALLOWConosco la giovane gentildonna. Ha buone doti. |
| EVANS | Settecento sterline e possipilità sono pone toti. |
| SHALLOW | [[motifs/well\|Bene]], andiamo a vedere l’onesto [[characters/master-page\|mastro Page]]. C’è [[characters/falstaff\|Falstaff]] là? |
| EVANS | Ti dirò una pugia? Tisprezzo un pugiardo come tisprezzo uno che è falso, o come tisprezzo uno che non è fero. Tio penetica la vostra casa qui I |
| *(didascalia)* | PAGE dall’interno Chi è là? |
| EVANS | Qui c’è la penetizione di Tio e il vostro amico, e [[characters/justice-shallow\|il giudice Shallow]], e qui il giovane mastro Slender, che forse fi racconterà un’altra storia se le cose fanno di vostro gratimento. |
| *(didascalia)* | Entra [[characters/master-page\|mastro Page]] |

| SHALLOW | Signore, vi ringrazio. Per il sì e per il no, davvero. |

| SLENDER | Per questo cappello, allora, l’ebbe quello dalla faccia rossa. Ché, sebbene io non ricordi che cosa feci quando mi faceste ubriacare, pure non sono del tutto un asino. |

| EVANS | Si dice ‘i suoi cinque sensi’. Ohibò, che ignoranza! |

| EVANS | Che Tio mi ciutichi, questa è una mente virtuosa. |
| SIR JOHN | Udite tutte queste cose negate, signori, l’udite. |

| SHALLOW | Sì, questo è il punto, signore. |

| SHALLOW | Cugino Abraham Slender, puoi [[motifs/love\|amarla]]? |
| SLENDER | [[concepts/hope\|Spero]], signore, di fare come conviene a chi voglia agire secondo ragione. |

| SLENDER | Farò cosa più grande di quella, a vostra richiesta, cugino, entro ogni ragione. |

Ma se non c’è grande [[motifs/love\|amore]] al principio, pure il cielo può diminuirlo con miglior conoscenza, quando saremo sposati e avremo più occasione di conoscerci l’un l’altro. [[concepts/hope\|Spero]] che con la familiarità crescerà più disprezzo. Ma se voi dite ‘sposala’, io la sposerò. Su ciò sono liberamente dissoluto, e dissolutamente. |
| EVANS | È una risposta di fera tiscrezione, salvo che la colpa sta nella parola ‘dissolutamente’. La parola è, secondo il nostro senso, ‘risolutamente’. Il suo senso è puono. |

| SLENDER | Sì, altrimenti vorrei essere impiccato, sì. |

| ANNE | Sì, davvero, signore. |

| SLENDER | Preferisco essere scortese che importuno. Fate torto a voi stessa, davvero, sì. |

| Chi parla | Battuta |
|---|---|

| SIMPLE | [[motifs/well\|Bene]], signore. |
| *(didascalia)* | EVANS No, c’è ancora di petter. Dalle questa lettera, perché è una tonna che ha tutta la conoscenza di madonna [[characters/anne-page\|Anne Page]].
